Catalog
Toh 1420 (Tōhoku catalogue; also cited as D 1420 of the Derge edition) · Derge Tengyur · Tantra commentaries (rgyud 'grel) · Tantra

About this text

The Padminī, or Lotus-Bearer, known in Sanskrit as the Śrī Saṃvarodaya Mahātantrarājasya Padminī Nāma Pañcikā and in Tibetan as dPal sdom pa 'byung ba'i rgyud kyi rgyal po chen po'i dka' 'grel padma can, is a commentary on the difficult points of the Saṃvarodaya Tantra, one of the principal scriptures of the Cakrasaṃvara cycle of Buddhist yoginī tantra. Composed by the Indian scholar and adept, the monk Ratnarakṣita, it works through the tantra chapter by chapter, explaining its maṇḍalas, mantras, rites and offerings, and above all its teachings on the generation and completion stages, the inner channels and drops, the four joys, and the inseparability of emptiness and compassion embodied in the deities Heruka and Vajravārāhī. It is Toh 1420 in the Tengyur, where it belongs to the section of tantra commentaries. According to its colophon, it was rendered into Tibetan by the Tibetan translator, the monk Lodrö Ten, building on an earlier translation by the author's direct disciple.

Translation

In the language of India: śrī saṃbarodaya mahātantrarājasya padminī nāma pañcikā In the language of Tibet: The Lotus-Bearer, a Commentary on the Difficult Points of the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Saṃvarodaya.

Homage to the glorious wheel of Cakrasaṃvara!

Source of every profound jewel without exception, sole treasury that sustains the lives of beings,
Truly relied upon by hosts of the awakened, abode of hundreds of manifold wonders,
Rich in true heroines, a body joined with nectar, the ground of all that is excellent and good:
The great Saṃvarodaya Tantra, an ocean of milk, is relied upon by the wise.

With the eye of understanding, its darkness dispelled
By the light of the lamp of my guru's kindness,
Whatever meaning of the Saṃvarodaya
I have seen, I shall write down here.

Though one like me who enters it cannot fathom its depth and its extent as they are,
Still, wishing to make virtue habitual, I undertake this explanation.
Even if one cannot touch the very bottom of that treasury of waters,
Do not those who enter it by the proper method gain the glory of the aim they seek?

Here, the Blessed One, the Buddha, intending without fail to deliver the three realms in their entirety, awakened to perfect and complete buddhahood, and then fully satisfied beings with the nectar of the Dharma, each according to their fortune. Moreover, for the sake of certain disciples who were not present at that time, this compilation of the Dharma was made. Compilation means arranging the Dharma that was heard into a treatise, in its connections and in its order, exactly as it was heard.

Now first of all, the compiler of the Blessed One's word sets forth the passage from "Thus have I heard" up to "requested" as a factor that draws listening people to engage. He does this to show that this treatise was not made up by himself, but was fully understood through his auditory consciousness from the very words spoken by the person who has become authoritative, the unequalled, the supreme among the holy, the sole Teacher of all beings, gods, asuras, humans and the rest; and so to show that he himself is authoritative. As it is said:

As a factor to draw the faithful to engage,
And to establish oneself as authoritative,
The teacher, the assembly and the witnesses,
The place and the time are definitely shown.
In the world as well, a compiler
Indicates the place and the time.
By declaring, "the lord of speakers,"
Authority is gained all the more.

If one asks, "Since no specific place and time are stated here, how are these to be known?", it should be answered: those alone are not what chiefly establishes certainty, for even without them, in some cases authority comes about through the lord alone. The line "indicates the place and the time" is said with regard to what is usual. In the Perfection system as well, since time is not a real entity, only the place was definitely shown, as being suitable to go to for merit, for sightseeing and the like. Here, however, because this teaching proceeds for whoever is to be trained, at whatever time and in whatever place, at that very time and in that very place, no determination of place can be made; and so it was taught only in general terms.

Alternatively, there are also cases in which place and time are specifically determined. For thus it is said: "For those of inferior inclination, the Blessed One taught the Śrāvaka Vehicle. For those inclined to the profound and for those whose minds are free of attachment, he made clear the Perfection system. And for the vessels of the supremely profound Dharma, at the glorious stūpa of Dhānyakaṭaka, on the full moon of the month of Caitra, he manifested the maṇḍala of glorious Dharmadhātuvāgīśvara and made clear all the treatises of the Mantra system." Here, however, in keeping with the ultimate meaning, that was not indicated, in order to dispel clinging to external objects as things apprehended.

Or else, the determination of place and time is not spoken of here in order to show that, for as long as saṃsāra lasts, he dwells within the palace of the vajra-jewel peak that lies inside each of the subtlest particles of all phenomena throughout the boundless expanse of space in all the ten directions without exception.

For by these words and the like, the defining character of the teaching, the causes that accomplish its activity, and all its qualities come to be expressed. That is: a teacher of such a kind, in such a place, to such an assembly gathered there, at the request of such a questioner, taught this Dharma. Therefore, thinking, "Without doubt there must be some special meaning here as well," the disciples who listen become deeply respectful toward hearing it and the rest. And statements of the qualities of the causes serve to illuminate the outstanding qualities of the result, just as, when something is accomplished through an activity such as cooking, one praises the qualities of the agent, the means, and so on.

Yet here the Teacher alone is both what is expressed and what expresses it. As it is said in the King of Tantras in Two Examinations:

I am the expounder, and I am the Dharma too;
I am the listener, together with my own assembly.

Therefore the expressing words, the subject expressed, the relation, the purpose and the purpose of that purpose should also be stated. Among these, this tantra consisting of thirty-three chapters is the expressing words. The Blessed One, glorious Cakrasaṃvara, whose nature is the two stages together with their ancillaries and who is the very embodiment of cause and result, is the subject expressed. The relation between them is that of expressing words and subject expressed. That the disciples come to understand the meaning of the tantra in their own mind-streams is the purpose of the treatise. That, by gradually abandoning the two obscurations, they accomplish the Great Seal (mahāmudrā), which is of benefit to all beings, is the purpose of the purpose. Such is the summarized meaning.

Now the meaning of the parts is also to be stated. "Thus" means exactly as it will be explained, with nothing omitted and nothing added; by this it is shown that the compiler himself has fully attained the power of retention (dhāraṇī). "I heard" and the rest show that he heard it directly, and this removes any suspicion of distortion, because it presents the meaning as it was spoken. Alternatively, it says, "What I heard is just what I witnessed directly; it is not something I made myself," and by this the supreme profundity of the Dharma is to be shown.

Or else it means, "I, for my part, heard it thus, while others heard it in other ways as well," because the modes of the Blessed One's teaching enter in many forms according to the force of each one's particular inclination.

"At one time" means, "In one single instant the Blessed One taught and I heard"; and by this it is shown that he himself has attained the inconceivable gateway of liberation. Or else it means, "at one time this, and at another time something else," that is, he heard a great deal. Or else it means, "Only at one time did I hear it thus, and never at any other time"; and this shows that the Dharma is hard to find.

"The Blessed One" is said because he possesses qualities such as lordship, and also because he has vanquished the māra of the afflictions and the others. By this the Teacher is rightly described as one who has fully realized the well-perfected and wholly pure accumulations of merit and wisdom, whose riches of his own welfare are entirely outstanding and ultimate, and who therefore has the power to accomplish the riches of others' welfare without exception.

"All" means that they are all, and they are also tathāgatas: that is, the emanation body, the Dharma body and the body of complete enjoyment, which are their body, speech and mind. That itself, because it is of the nature of great bliss and also because it is its cause, is the bhaga of Vajrayoginī. Or else, the vajra of the body, speech and mind of all the tathāgatas is the Blessed One Vajrasattva, and it is his yoginī and her bhaga. Or else, that itself is the bhaga, and it is in that. The word is plural because she is of a nature that gathers all the consorts together. Either way, the meaning is "within the palace that rests upon the source of phenomena."

By its being the abode of the unsurpassed play of the Dharma, of joy and of bliss, the excellence of the place is fully shown.

To show the excellence of the assembly, the text says "noble" and so on. They are noble because they conduct themselves far from sinful things. "One who brings delight to the qualities of the Buddha through samādhi and the like" is the noble Ānanda. "And others" refers to those free of attachment, of whom he is the first. Surrounded by these first, and likewise by the hosts of bodhisattvas such as the noble Avalokiteśvara.

If one objects, "How do those belonging to the Śrāvaka Vehicle enter here, since their attitude is free of attachment and their wisdom is inferior? For it is said:

A monk who dwells in meditative concentration,
A person who delights in logic,
One who dwells in an aged body:
To these one should not teach reality,"

then it should be answered: these are bodhisattvas who have taken the form of emanated śrāvakas; they are not actual śrāvakas. As it is said in the Uncommon Secret Yoga Tantra, "Ānanda is Samantabhadra," and so on.

If one asks, "In that case, since they are understood from the very presentation of the assembly of bodhisattvas, why are they shown again separately?", it is to make known that in the Mantra system the supreme practitioner is a monk. Thus too it is said:

A monk should be made the vajra holder;
He is supreme for menacing the wicked.

And Vajrapāṇipāda too declared that, compared with a lay master or a novice master, a master who is a monk is supreme. The meaning is that the prohibition is also reasonable with regard to those of inferior wisdom who are especially inclined toward the Śrāvaka Vehicle alone.

"Eighty koṭis": although they are in fact innumerable, this states the number of the principal ones. "Yogins" refers to the union of wisdom and means, that is, the samādhi in which calm abiding and insight are joined as a pair. Those who have it are "lords," because they have attained what is outstanding.

The excellence of the questioner is stated: "Vajrapāṇi" is he in whose hand, as though on his very palm, is the vajra that symbolizes the samādhi whose nature is the five wisdoms, the mirror-like wisdom and the rest. "Smiled" symbolizes the Teacher's intention. So that the assembly, having understood the intention that the tathāgatas wish to teach, will not be left in indifference, the buddhas, the blessed ones, display signs; just as, at the time of teaching the noble Perfection of Wisdom, he displayed to the noble Maitreya three kinds of indicative signs: gazing upon him, radiating rays of light, and a clearly manifest smile.

Although the noble Avalokiteśvara and the others were present, he gazed upon Vajrapāṇi because Vajrapāṇi is able to protect the sacred images and the holy Dharma, because he has authority to compile the Vajra Vehicle, and because he is able to tame those who are hard to tame.

"Rose from his seat" shows humility out of respect for the Dharma and a demeanor of deep faith, and it is also for the instruction of future disciples.

Moreover, by this very passage the summarized meaning of all the tantra is shown. Thus, that which makes clear and makes understood is tantra. It is of three kinds: the tantra of method, the tantra of cause, and the tantra of result. As it is said in the Later Tantra of the Guhyasamāja:

Tantra is said to mean continuity.
That tantra becomes threefold,
Divided into the basis,
The nature, and the inalienable.

Of these, the tantra of result is the Blessed One, glorious Vajradhara, the embodiment of union; it is spoken of in order to express the nature of the Teacher. Even if it would be fitting to state only the result connected with the practitioner, still it is spoken of only through what is similar to it, meaning, "When one is perfected, just such a result will be accomplished." Furthermore, in the Mantra system, although the practitioner's nature is established as the wholly pure buddha-essence, by focusing on it as free of stains and wholly pure, all adverse factors are abandoned, and so the desired goal comes to be accomplished. As it is said:

Buddhahood itself is the cause of accomplishment;
In every aspect, union with the buddhas.

Likewise Jñānapāda says:

The very nature of Samantabhadra,
Meditated upon with effort, is great awakening.

The buddha-essence, too, is taught through three reasons: because the Dharma body of the perfect Buddha pervades, because it is inseparable from suchness, and because of the capacity to accomplish buddhahood. As it is said:

Because the body of the perfect Buddha radiates,
Because suchness is undivided,
And because all always have the potential,
Embodied beings possess the buddha-essence.

Therefore the Blessed One himself is both the result and the path, and the tantra of cause is to be explained in relation to this.

The syllable e is the goddess's source of phenomena. The syllable vaṃ is the vajra. mayā means union. śrutaṃ means melting and falling. "At one time" means that in the moment of coemergent joy, whose nature is complete and in which the whole of the uncommon body, speech and mind is closely focused, the Blessed One dwells as a body inseparable from the great bliss of the vajra of the awakening mind; that is, he abides with a nature that pervades all phenomena. As it is said:

e is declared to be the bhaga;
The letter vaṃ is understood as the vajra;
mayā is what sets in motion;
śrutaṃ is held to be the two.
The form of the fluid becomes the Blessed One;
Its bliss is understood as the beloved.
Its nature is the Dharma body and the body of complete enjoyment:
This is the defining mark of Vajrasattva.

Or else:

The syllable e is to be known as earth:
The action seal, the Lady of Action,
Abides in the emanation wheel at the navel,
In its sixty-four petals.
The syllable vaṃ is to be known as water:
The Dharma seal, Māmakī,
Abides in the Dharma wheel at the heart,
In a lotus of eight petals.
The syllable mā is definitely taught as fire:
The great seal, Pāṇḍaravāsinī,
Abides in the enjoyment wheel at the throat,
In a lotus of twice eight petals.
The syllable yā is declared to be wind:
The pledge seal, Tārā,
Abides in the wheel of great bliss,
A lotus of thirty-two petals.
śrutaṃ is declared to be the coemergent,
Distinguished by the division into two.

The rest is as before.

"The noble Ānanda and others" refers to the presence of the supreme joy that transcends the world, and the rest. "The noble Avalokiteśvara" means that the eighty natural states, such as desire, have become of one taste with emptiness and have been turned into qualities through the yoga of great bliss; by this he stands at the center of the lords. As it is said: "The afflictions become limbs of awakening." Vajrapāṇi is nondual wisdom. Gazing is experience. "Rose from his seat" means rose from that samādhi, or else from the secret wheel. "Placed his upper robe over his shoulder" means having brought it to the supreme limb. "Placed his right kneecap on the ground": in the symbolic language of the tathāgatas this is the wholly pure body of complete enjoyment, and "on the ground" means on the nature of reality. "Joined his palms" means that existence and peace have become inseparable. "To the Blessed One" means to that very nondual wisdom, the venerable one. "Requested" means urging toward the benefit of beings, without any mental engagement. This repeated explanation is not without relevance.

The tantra of method, too, is explained by this very passage. What is called method is the cooperating cause that belongs to the tantra of cause, which serves as the substantial cause. Or else, all three tantras should be understood as included in what this very explanation states. The nature and the distinctions of cause and result will be explained immediately.

If one asks, "Of what is to be produced, what is to be transformed, and what is to be attained, which is Vajradhara?" Suppose he is something to be produced. Then he would fall on the same side as destruction. For it is said, "Monks, whatever has the nature of arising, all of that has the nature of ceasing." Therefore he would again become a being of saṃsāra; and being partial, he would not be omniscient, because when he knows one other state, having excluded a place and time, it is possible that he does not know another place and time. Therefore it would follow that the liberated and the unliberated are no different.

The position that he is something to be transformed is no different from the position that he is something to be produced. The convention of transformation applies when, in some single thing that has states, one state is reversed and another state comes into play, as with gold and the like taking the form of an earring and so on. And that is impossible in the view of Buddhists who teach momentary destruction, because even things that are extremely similar have the nature of perishing moment by moment, with nothing continuing through.

The position that he is something to be attained has many faults. A result that is to be attained has no need of cooperating and substantial causes. Moreover, what is called attainment is the attaining of what was not attained. And that is reasonable only for what is distinct, because meeting and separating occur exclusively between distinct things. Even so, one would be called "possessing buddhahood," but not "a buddha," just as one is said to possess cattle. If there is no distinction, then it is simply attained, and not something to be attained. Whatever is not distinct from something is its nature, like the heat of fire. Therefore all sentient beings would be buddhas without any cultivation of the path at all, because there is no effort in applying a means to make manifest what is one's very nature.

Suppose one says, "Even though it is their nature, conventionally it is obscured by the cataract of beginningless unreal conceptuality, and so it becomes as though distinct from its nature, which possesses full clarity; just as the clarity of the sky and the like is obscured by clouds and the like." That is not so, because in both cases, since they abide as a single nature of reality, there cannot reasonably be something obscured and something that obscures. Between the light and the hardness residing in a gem there is no such thing as obscured and obscurer. Those contradictions are reasonable only between a property and a property-bearer that are distinct.

Moreover, if what is called being obscured means having a nature that is not ascertained as buddhahood, then whatever abides in something without being ascertained does not thereby give up performing its own function there: fire does not become non-burning even though it is not ascertained as burning.

For as long as they remain on the body of a child or the like, gems, mantras and medicines that the child does not recognize still perform their function.

Furthermore, even if conceptual thoughts, by obscuring the seeing of the true meaning, become obscurers in the manner of darkness, how could buddhahood, which is of the manner of supreme and unequalled light, become something obscured? Light is not obscured by darkness; light is what destroys darkness.

Suppose one says: "If that is so, then one never needs to abandon the obscurations or to meditate. Moreover, how could these three, the mind's having abandoned its stains, its being obscured, and its becoming the antidote, reasonably belong to a single partless nature at a single time? For an action upon itself is contradictory. And at different times it is utterly unreasonable, because when one is present the other cannot be."

All of that, every bit of it, is without substance, because the meaning of the scriptures is not a domain to be traversed by logic. As it is said in the Ornament of the Sūtras:

Dependent, uncertain, not pervasive,
Conventional and wearisome,
Relying on the childish: so logic is held to be.
Therefore this is not its domain.

The same is said in the Glorious Heruka Abhidhāna:

It cannot be overturned
By the reasonings of all the common tantras;
It is to be grasped by faith alone,
Not by the eye or the hand.

And in the Mahāyāna Uttaratantra:

The ultimate of the self-arisen ones
Is to be entered here by faith alone.
The blazing disk of the sun
Is not seen by those without eyes.

And Sarahapāda too says:

Not the domain of the intellect, not the domain of speech,
The account of reality taught by the guru enters deep and far.
Gradually it brings compassion and the other white qualities,
And in the heart of the faithful it bestows the state itself.

Suppose one says, "In that case, the claims of the Vedas and the like would likewise not be contradicted." That is not so, because those are untenable even by supramundane valid cognition. Here that is impossible: since experience is realized through the instructions that come down through the lineage of gurus, how could the two be alike? And faith here is conviction, clarity and longing, based on confidence in what is authoritative by reason of its existence, its qualities and its capacity; it is not, as with other, foolish people, devoid of reasoning.

Nevertheless, even granting the objection, it should be answered. First, as to the claim that whatever is produced is destroyed: is its destruction merely the exhaustion of a continuum, like a lamp and the like? Or, when another continuum arises, does the former continuum cease, as with a seed and the like and a sprout and the like? Or does a similar result arise anew, again and again, moment by moment? It is not the first, because mind, being as it were stable, cannot be cut off in any way. Even a lamp is established as lasting a long time if, in a place free of disturbance, there is oil, a wick and the like; what need, then, to speak of the Blessed One, vast as the sky, stainless, and endowed with the accumulations of merit and wisdom? In the world too, as long as there is even a little merit, the continuum of beings is seen not to be cut off. For that very reason there is no fault of his abiding in only one place, because he pervades through the two accumulations.

As for the claim that, being partial, he lacks omniscience: like the knowledge gained through a young girl's pratisena divination, and like the knowledge in a true dream, the wisdom of the yogin knows without error the things that abide in all times and all directions. And it is not merely partial; otherwise there would be no realization of anything long past and the like.

The second option is also impossible for the Blessed One, because there is no cause that could produce a dissimilar result. The two kinds of thorough affliction are its cause, and the Blessed One has abandoned them. Nor is it accepted that his continuum declines through the force of unwanted conditions, nor through his own wish, because that is impossible for one who displays the continuum of his emanation body.

The third is not unacceptable, because it is accepted for the body of complete enjoyment, which is uninterrupted and permanent. Therefore, in relation to the body of complete enjoyment and the emanation body, his being something produced is also accepted; in relation to the Dharma body, he is something to be attained.

Nor is there any occasion for the faults that were stated. Thus, first, it was said that attainment is contradictory where there is no distinction. Here, attainment means realization; it is not grasping with the hand. And so, even if they are inseparable, there is no fault. Even something that abides in a thing as its very nature is not necessarily ascertained; just as momentariness and the like, for as long as they have not been ascertained, are investigated as unascertained.

Moreover, are these things being said in relation to ultimate truth or in relation to conventional truth? On the first, there is nothing whatever that we do not accept: in ultimate truth there is no thing to be attained, no attainer and no attainment, since ultimately no phenomena at all are apprehended. On the second, the objection contradicts direct perception. For through an alteration of the mind, something that abides in its own essence in one way is seen in another way: just as from disordered bile, or from eating datura and the like, one's own body and the like, though not spinning, seem to spin. And just as, when the cause of that adventitious delusion has subsided, one speaks of attaining one's own body and the like, abiding in their natural state, and one even applies means in order to attain it, so too here: what is unreasonable? And by this very fact of its not being ascertained, the claim that it performs its function merely by virtue of existing is also refuted; that is unreasonable in every way.

As for what was said, "How could light be obscured by darkness?" and so on, there is nothing wrong in that either. For there is no difference in the way buddhahood and conceptual thought are what the words "light" and "darkness" refer to. Buddhahood is called light because it is able to reverse what is adverse, because conceptual thoughts cannot reverse it, and because it is a continuum that has understood everything knowable. And conceptual thoughts are of the nature of darkness, because they always cling to what is unreal.

If one asks, "How can you call contradictory things a single property-bearer?", what is unreasonable about what is plainly seen? Grasping at permanence and the like is the adverse side, and momentariness and the like is the side of the antidote, yet there are not many property-bearers. With regard to the momentary nature of mind, grasping it as having its own essence and apprehending it wrongly are that same mind. For that very reason it is said:

In the pure nature of the mind,
The stains are adventitious.

As for your phrase "in a single partless mind," who has deceived you? In unexamined convention, mind is a captivating thing, both one and many, and its properties are many. Ultimately it is neither one nor many. For that very reason the emanation of the many forms of the maṇḍala wheel and the like, and properties such as the five wisdoms, the three bodies, the powers and the fearlessnesses, are not distinct, because their mutual exclusion is not established, because a single wisdom runs through them all, and because the yoga and the bliss are one. Nor are they non-distinct, because their characteristics differ and they perform various functions. If one accepted ultimately real entities, the fault of falling into the positions of distinct, non-distinct and so on would arise; but not for what does not abide in any way at all, like a dream and the like. Enough, for this would go too far.

Now, to make clear the meaning of what is stated in summary, it is to be explained. In order to make his request, the requester himself, addressing the one who was its object, summarized the meaning comprised by method and spoke, in the words beginning "to hear."

There, at the very outset, the generation stage is requested in order to show the sequence of meditation through the tantra of method, to summarize what is to be purified, and to make clear the wholly pure nature. "Generation" means generating, being born and arising. "By this application" refers to yoga; that is its defining mark. Then comes the completion stage, which arises from its method: the stages of generation, completion, accomplishment and union. Its cause is the nature of the result of the generation stage; it is spoken of in this way by imputing the result upon the cause. It is twofold, divided into self-blessing and luminosity. Vajra recitation runs through both stages. Through the division into the generation stage and the completion stage, the stages are just two. As it is said:

The Dharma taught by the Vajra Holder
Rests truly on two stages:
The generation stage
And the completion stage.

Therefore, as the principal matter, the two stages alone were asked about first; the remaining questions are their ancillaries. This is what is meant by "all aspects in a single saṃvara": the division into all aspects is a single aspect; that which has no second is saṃvara, that is, what is gathered together.

Here, union is the tantra of result. Self-blessing and pure luminosity, together with their ancillaries, are the tantra of cause. The generation stage together with its ancillaries is the tantra of method.

After that, since these stages of generation and completion depend on the aggregate of the elements and what derives from the elements, he asked about the characteristics of the five elements: "How are wind, fire and water?" That is, how are wind, fire, water, earth and space; how are their characteristics? Here too, in order to state that one should turn from reliance on the letter to reliance on the meaning, and to make clear that the tantra is understood through the guru's kindness alone, grammatical gender and the like should be regarded as reversed.

In both stages one should meditate on deities whose nature is the five wisdoms; so he asked about the characteristics of the five aspects. The question about the characteristics of the six objects, though separated by intervening matter, should be understood as coming at the end of the question about the five elements. Then "six objects" means: what are the characteristics of the six objects and of their subjects? Here the subject is not taken in by the word "object" itself; it is implied because that word is a relational term.

One should meditate on the deity endowed with the blessing of body, speech and mind; so he asked, "the blessing of body, speech and mind." One should fully understand both the blessing of body, speech and mind and the purity, outer and inner; so he asked about this, saying "abiding outside and within." The unsurpassed outer and inner purity, adorned with the thirty-two major marks and the eighty minor marks, and also the emanations with various arms, faces and the like: how do these come about through the generation stage? He asked about this in the words beginning "of the deity."

For one abiding in the generation stage, entry into the completion stage comes through nothing other than vajra recitation. That does not occur without knowing the five paths; those do not occur without knowing the nature of the body; and that comes from nothing other than the nature and number of the channels. So he asked in order about vajra recitation and the rest, which are ancillaries of both stages, in the words beginning "moon and sun."

In the second stage as well, one must accomplish the entity of the bodily mass, the equality of body, speech and mind; so he asked about it: "How is that bodily mass?" "Of the body" means of this body. In "mass," the word is given without a case ending; it should be understood as "its characteristics."

The meditator of the generation stage must certainly perform the gathering pūja, and in order to guard the pledges there he must know the signs; so he asked about these in the words beginning "pledge."

The yogin who is to ascertain the wheel, that is, the wheel of channels, must also know, outwardly and inwardly, the arrangement of the sacred places and the rest, which are of outer and inner nature, and the arrangement of the grounds beginning with the Very Joyful; so he asked about these in order in the words beginning "the signs of the places." The meaning is: "How many are the signs of the places and the rest?"

The yogin who travels around the outer places and the rest wishes to see the yoginīs: will he come to see them? This is asked in "How will the signs be seen?"

The yogin who has performed recitation and the fire offering should accomplish a myriad of rites for his own aims; so it says "How your twelve rites" and so on. The meaning is: "What are the twelve?"

Only when a mantra is recited in accordance with the rite will it bring accomplishment; so he asked about the characteristics of mantra recitation.

That, moreover, should be accomplished only with a rosary on which the rite has been performed; so he asked about the rosary. "Of what kind, the rosary proper" means "What kind of rosary is proper?"

Recitation itself, too, should be done by one who knows the characteristics of the mantra; so he asked about that. In "How many are the characteristics of recitation?", "how many" means how many kinds, and "recitation" means the mantra that is recited; it is its characteristics that are asked.

All of these should be done through deity yoga; so he asked about deity yoga in the words beginning "how many." In the yoga of the deity's form, the deity dwells encircled by the maṇḍala as its retinue; so the meaning is: "How many maṇḍalas made up of deities are there in this tantra?"

Deity yoga, moreover, is not without the mantras for protection, for planting the ritual dagger, and the rest; so he asked about that: "How are the mantras of accomplishment to be regarded?" Mantras that are the cause of accomplishment are the mantras of accomplishment.

Since this very tantra is to be meditated upon free of obstructors, here one who abides in the manner of the yoginīs should perform the worship of maidens through recitation and supreme meditative concentration; on which lunar day this is done; and on that day how the torma with liquor is to be offered: these three he asked in order in the words beginning "maiden." "Maidenhood" means the maiden. "How many" means "on which."

At all times, so that one may be free of obstructors to this very practice, the pledge pills should be taken; so he asked about the five nectars and the five hooks.

The yogin who meditates on the wheel of deities in its elaborate form should draw a maṇḍala of colored powders for the sake of taking disciples into his care and so on. That is preceded by laying out the lines; that in turn is preceded by purifying the site; and these too are preceded by the protection wheel. So he asked about these four in order.

What kind of person should the disciple take as guru, and what kind of disciple should the guru in turn properly accept? So he asked about the characteristics of the guru and the disciple. "Master," given without a case ending, should take the ending of the first case, singular. "By which" should be understood as "with the characteristics"; it is the third case in the sense of accompaniment.

One who is empowered according to the rite is entitled to listen to the tantra and so on; so it is said in the words beginning "how many." The meaning is: "How many kinds of empowerment are there?" Only the fourth is the principal one and the result; so he asked about that.

The yogin, having come to know the signs of timely and untimely death for himself and for others, should strive to avert it as far as he is able; so he asked about these.

One who knows the characteristics of the four times is skilled in accomplishing mantras and the rest; so he asked about that.

Likewise, one skilled in the arrangement of the introductory setting will engage in virtue; so he asked about the setting out of the introductory setting.

"At what time, and by reciting the mantra how many times, is accomplishment gained?" This is said in the words beginning "knowing the time."

Even though he has attained heat and the rest, a yogin who lacks the conduct does not gain accomplishment; so it is said in the words beginning "conduct."

The yoginī tantras and the rest are taught according to differences of inclination, and fixing their number too serves to inspire faith; so this is the fourth question.

Images and the like, made by oneself or by others, are not fit to be worshiped if they lack consecration; so he asked about consecration.

Because it is a limb of accomplishing mantras and the rest, he asked about the fire offering; the fire offering is itself a sacrificial offering.

Even apart from the fire offering and the like, there are ways in which mantra alone brings accomplishment; for the sake of some who seek minor accomplishments, he asked about that.

If one cannot reverse white hair and the like by the power of yoga, one should apply medicinal preparations; so he asked about rejuvenation (rasāyana).

Because it is the chief of the ancillary pledges, he asked about brewing liquor.

Mantras passed on merely from ear to ear become corrupted in their vowel signs and the like; so, for the sake of extracting the mantras, he asked two questions. "The source of mantras is from this": the meaning is the source of mantras, the wheel in which the vowels and consonants are arranged.

One who abides in the generation stage should also tame some by subjugating them and some by taking them into his care; so he asked about these.

The accomplishment of the Great Seal (mahāmudrā) belongs to those who meditate on reality, and not to others. Reality, moreover, is the inseparability of emptiness and compassion. Emptiness too is of two kinds. So, in order to elaborate the completion stage, he asked these four in order. "Realities": since they are of four kinds, divided as the reality of the self and so on, this is to be connected with "characteristics."

"How are emptiness and compassion to be meditated upon as inseparable?" This is said in "of the full knowledge of all phenomena": the meditation that is the cause of fully knowing all phenomena is the meditation on all phenomena; "please explain what its characteristics are."

There are no further questions, for by these alone the two stages together with their ancillaries are complete. The incidental topics, such as the characteristics of a suitable vessel, are also asked within these very questions.

Thus, the tantra having been summarized by way of requests through these sixty-two topics, "I shall explain the meaning of just this much" is what is meant by "the condensed meaning of the tantra is taught."

This is the explanation of the first chapter.

Beginning from here, in order to teach in sequence the meaning of the questions that were asked, the text runs from the second chapter through to the thirty-third. "Then the Blessed One said" are the words of the compiler. "Excellent, excellent!" praises Vajrapāṇi for having made his request completely and in full.

That very point is stated in "secret": because you ask, completely, about a matter that is not the domain of any of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. Having praised him in this way, he spoke in order to teach the topic at hand, in the words beginning "Then." The word "then" means "without interval." "From here" is to be construed thus: from this beginning up to the end of the chapter, I shall explain the generation stage.

The generation stage, because it corresponds to the four modes of birth, should be meditated upon in order to purify them, and also in order to enter the completion stage by way of the sequence of the isolation of body, speech and mind. Therefore he teaches those very modes of birth in order, in the words beginning "just as."

Among these, the deity completed from moon and sun through the sequence of seed syllable and sign is birth from an egg. That which emerges from the awakening mind melted through the churning of the vajra and lotus of father and mother is birth from a womb. That which arises from moon and seed syllable alone is birth from warmth and moisture. Some say that emanation is birth from warmth and moisture. That which arises in an instant from luminosity, without seed syllable and the rest, is miraculous birth. Miraculous birth itself is also to be set forth as the profound generation stage and, on examination, as the completion stage.

Just as a compassionate physician, faced with another's dying, first points out the disease together with its basis and then points out the medicine, so here at the outset he points out just the three kinds of thorough affliction.

Furthermore, according to the principle stated in:

Whatever is the limit of nirvāṇa
is also the limit of saṃsāra;
between the two not even the subtlest difference
exists in the least,

he first teaches only the completely pure nature. When the completely pure nature is fully ascertained, freedom from stains is complete purity. That is stated in the glorious King of the Two Examinations:

These phenomena are nirvāṇa;
through delusion they take the nature of saṃsāra.
Without bewilderment, saṃsāra is pure:
in saṃsāra itself is nirvāṇa.

If one asks, "Is this teaching of the Dharma not present in continents other than Jambudvīpa as well? And if it is not, how is that?", he answers in the words beginning "east." "They live well with great wealth" means they live well through karma that causes one to live well with great wealth. They are "dull" because they are themselves ignorant of what to affirm and what to exclude; "bewildered" because they cannot grasp the meaning of what others say. These are the very ones who cannot discriminate.

If one asks how it is in the Jambudvīpas, he answers in the words beginning "Jambudvīpa." The plural is used for the singular; or else it is said with reference to the Jambudvīpas of other four-continent worlds. "Land of karma" means the place of good and bad deeds; the other continents are for the most part lands where the fruit of ripening is experienced. Teaching the divisions of karma, he speaks in the words beginning "good deeds": good deeds and bad deeds are each of three kinds. That this Jambudvīpa is, for the most part, the land where results corresponding to their cause and dominant results are experienced is stated in the words beginning "former birth."

If one asks how this alone is the land of karma, he answers in the words beginning "arrogance": here alone beings are tormented by terrible arrogance, miserliness, malicious intent, deceit, pretense, overweening pride, desire, aversion, delusion, aging and sickness.

If one asks, "Even among the people of Jambudvīpa, is it not the case that this was not taught everywhere?", he answers in the words beginning "of Jambudvīpa." "Born in the central region" refers to those born in the central land. Here too, depending on former virtuous karma, beings are of three kinds, divided into dull, middling and sharp; this is stated in the words beginning "dull." He gives the summary in the words beginning "as a human." Therefore, in Jambudvīpa, to obtain a human birth free from the eight unfree states, to leave the household life, to go forth in the teaching of the Tathāgata, and to attain meditative absorption are as extremely hard to find as finding a supreme treasure.

If one asks how it is that those endowed with the qualities of beings of the first eon, of the intermediate existence, of the realm of entities and the rest are not liberated, he answers in the words beginning "like an illusion." "Therefore, previously done karma" means: for that reason, through previously done karma, they pass away and are born.

If one asks how long and in what manner one remains in the intermediate existence after passing away, and how from there one finds rebirth-linking in another destiny, he teaches it in order in the words beginning "assemblage." The assemblage is the totality of the causes of birth. As long as they have not come together, one passes away every seven days and is born again and again, remaining right there.

If one asks what form one has while remaining there, he says: "It is seen as having the form of a being of that existence," that is, it is like the existence in which one is going to be born. "With the strength of utter delight, quite certainly" qualifies the action. "Enters into the path of the mouth" means it enters by way of the mouth.

Then, if one asks how, from the intermediate existence itself, one passes over and is born, he answers in the words beginning "horse." "Horse-rider" means one mounted on a horse; in the same way, consciousness mounted on wind is like one riding a mount. Therefore, following the principle that where one is present the rest are present, the intention is that there exists a cause for the formation of a body whose nature is consciousness. Or else the meaning is that it enters relying on the wind of the father's breath as it moves.

How it enters is not known; so he says, in the words beginning "very swiftly," and the intention is: like fire blazing up in cotton through a fire-crystal when it is struck by the rays of the sun.

If one asks at what instant it enters, he answers in the words beginning "two and." The meaning is: at that instant when the wheel of channels is stirred, moon and sun are made to melt, and supreme joy is fully attained. "In the form of a drop" means in the form of space.

He teaches that even in the stages of the kalala and the rest there is a blessing into the five tathāgatas, whose nature is the five wisdoms and which are the cause of the purification of the five aggregates, in the words beginning "kalala": these are the meditation on the fivefold purity itself. In the words beginning "kalala," the kalala is the two elements mixed together, slippery.

The arbuda has the shape of a bubble, hollow inside, like a bubble on water. The peśī is the kalala itself become somewhat firm. The ghana is like flesh, since it has very soft skin. "Becoming firm" means that the five which will be the causes of the head, the hands and the feet develop; and there, further, are urine, blood and semen. "A mere lump" means that the nutritive essences and the rest have come together. "Mixed" means excrement. These are blessed by Akṣobhya and the rest.

Having explained by these the sequence of womb birth, he speaks of the completely pure nature and of the support of semen and blood in the words beginning "two channels." "In the center of the birthplace" means in the place below the navel. "Those" are the channels. "The very nature of the dharmadhātu": because they have the nature of the avadhūtī.

He speaks of the sequence of entering the womb in the words beginning "karma." Karma itself is the seed, because it is the cause of production. The meaning is that, turned around by a wind in keeping with that karma and made to face downward, it is led to face the door of the source of phenomena, and so is born.

When the gandharva being is going to become a male, at the time of rebirth-linking it enters with jealousy toward the father; when a female, toward the mother. Therefore a male among them remains in the womb facing the mother's back, and a female the reverse; this he teaches in the words beginning "right." That these are known by signs he teaches in the words beginning "seed." From "karma" up to "always becomes" is a digression.

That the body is born from the two elements of father and mother he teaches by distinguishing what corresponds to each of those two causes, in the words beginning "water element." The summary is in the words beginning "thus": arising in the aggregates comprising six is "having a body comprising six." If one reads "the body arises," the meaning is the same. He teaches that this also has the nature of the five aggregates in the words beginning "form."

He states the result of realizing all of these in the words beginning "person." "The birth of a person" means the formation of an existence. "Knowing" means having known. By this he shows clearly that things which arise, abide and perish like an illusion, a dream and the like are not a basis for grasping at "I" and so on. That is stated:

Whatever is conjured by illusion,
whatever is conjured by illusions,
one should examine where it has come from
and where it goes.

For that very reason, knowing alone is the cause of buddhahood. So too Carpaṭipāda says:

One whose mind has been taught that it is illusion
is not bound by its things;
a mind in which existence and nirvāṇa are stilled
becomes a vessel for the nondual accomplishment.

This is the explanation of the second chapter.

To answer the question about the completion stage, he spoke the words beginning "Then." That in both stages the wheel of aggregates, elements and sense sources itself is to be meditated upon as having the nature of the three bodies is stated in the words beginning "of the body."

In the generation stage, the emanation body is the process ending in the worship and praise of the wheel of deities dwelling within the celestial palace on the summit of Mount Meru, completed through the sequence of the maṇḍalas of wind and the rest arisen from the syllable yaṃ and the others, and in the tasting of the nectar. Entering into luminosity is the Dharma body. Rising from it in an instant is the enjoyment body. These too are of two kinds, divided into those with stains and those without stains. Those with stains are to be posited as having the nature of self-blessing; those without stains, as having the nature of union. For the completion stage, the text itself will explain it.

"The Dharma and enjoyment bodies" shows the neuter gender. With these as indication, the emanation body too is to be understood.

He teaches the meaning that was stated in detail in the words beginning "of the body." That itself is the maṇḍala of the body: maṇḍa is the essence, the body of great bliss; la is that which takes it. "Taught" means taught in the Guhyasamāja and the others; and it is also shown elsewhere in this tantra itself, where that is taught.

To show the order, namely that one of sharp faculties who has gained stability through the generation stage enters the completion stage, he spoke the words beginning "generation." A person of dull faculties should first make clear to conceptual thought the entire wheel of deities, completed gradually through the sequence of the four modes of birth, womb birth and the rest. One of middling faculties gathers all the aspects at once, in a single instant, as in miraculous birth. One of sharp faculties, for his part, resolves that the world itself, consisting of the stable and the moving, is by its nature established as the form of the deity, of one taste with emptiness and compassion.

Just as in the generation stage, immediately after all phenomena have been rendered without intrinsic nature, the wheel of aggregates, elements and sense sources itself becomes the wheel of deities, differentiated into support and supported, through the sequence of the maṇḍalas of wind and the rest arisen from the syllable yaṃ and the others; so too this wheel of the three realms, immediately after its destruction, has the nature of a wheel of support and supported, consisting of the container world and the world of beings completed gradually, and it is also of one taste with emptiness. That is stated by Āryadevapāda:

This is the celestial palace, not the three worlds; these are Victors, not living creatures;
this is the Lord of the Wheel, not a man; these are not sense objects, not faculties, not earth and the rest,
nor are they form and the rest: because their nature is the nature of reality, they belong to the maṇḍala.
Know all this variety as the wheel of the maṇḍala. Mind, why are you deluded?

Sarahapāda too says:

These women themselves are to be known as the host of the glorious vajra consorts;
these men themselves are to be known as the glorious deities of the maṇḍala;
your own bodies themselves are to be known as the great glorious vajra bearers.
Thus, all of you, this drama of beings is luminosity, established by its own nature.

These divisions into dull and the rest also occur even within a single person, through the progression of familiarization, just as childhood and the other stages are distinguished in one and the same person. And the distinction between different persons is like a child and others who are clearly different persons. Nevertheless, even the dull and the middling should focus on this yoga in the state outside meditative equipoise; as it is said:

With the yoga outside equipoise,
meditate on everything as having the nature of the Buddha.

He teaches these in the words beginning "of the sharp." If one asks how it is that they do not focus on it gradually, he answers with the words beginning "instant"; this is the brief teaching. The detailed teaching is in the words beginning "three realms."

"By the mantra oṃ āḥ hūṃ": in the sense that the three realms are comprised in them, they are to be designated as body, speech and mind; and because they are inseparable from these, the yogin's body, speech and mind themselves are the maṇḍala. That is stated in "in heaven, on the earth, and beneath the earth," which means "of heaven, of the earth's surface and of what lies beneath the earth." "Mantra" means by way of suchness. "To be designated" means to make manifest.

He states the result of these in the words beginning "all." "The union of all heroes" means likewise the one who is in complete union with the host of ḍākinīs. "Its Supreme Bliss (Cakrasaṃvara)" is intended to mean the Blessed One and the Blessed Lady. As it is said: "Vajraḍāka, the supreme of bliss." What the King of Tantras in Two Examinations says,

Because bliss arises from the great elements,
therefore bliss is not reality,

is so because the intent here is the bliss that is inseparable from emptiness.

He teaches these in detail in the words beginning "four elements." With these the emanation body is taught. If one asks how the aspect and the other are not separate, the answer is in the words beginning "suchness." Or else: the four elements, the five aggregates and the six objects are the Blessed Lady herself, whose form comprises all the goddesses, and wisdom is the Blessed One. They should not be imagined as separate. The reason they are not separate is stated in the words beginning "suchness."

That is stated in the words beginning "selfless": the variety itself, which has the nature of suchness, is Nairātmyā, and she is embraced by the vajra, which has the nature of great bliss and great compassion. These are summed up in the words beginning "union." The meaning is: therefore the maṇḍala whose nature is union is accomplished spontaneously.

If one objects, "Even if it is accomplished by its own nature, where does that union become clear, since one is deluded by conceptual thoughts of many kinds?", he answers in the words beginning "mind." Or else: even in the state of accomplishment, for any yogin of this union whatsoever, "engaging, withdrawing and the rest," that is, the various intentions and the various conducts of engaging and withdrawing, are conceptual thought, and that too has the very nature of union. That is stated by Sarahapāda:

Where water has entered into water,
there it becomes of equal taste.
Faults and virtues being the nature of mind,
fool, there is no opposing side at all.

If one asks why this is so, he answers in the words beginning "all aspects." "In this, everywhere supreme of aspects" means supreme in all aspects.

Even so, one may ask: how is there oneness of taste with emptiness even in the very nature of sublime bliss, in which the faculties and all aspects are supremely adorned, having understood and attained the non-referential great bliss of one taste with suchness? He answers in the words beginning "entity and." For "having the nature of entity and non-entity," what remains to be supplied is: "having made it an entity, it will constantly arise there."

If one asks how these are to be done, he answers in the words beginning "unsuperimposed." Superimposition is the excessive clinging, "it is just this"; that in which it is absent is likewise unsuperimposed. Mental engagement is conceptual thought: "this is so, and not so." Because that is absent, there is no mental engagement. It is only through superimposition and mental engagement that these, entity and non-entity, are posited as distinct. In its essential nature, it is the permanent dharmakāya; and it arises from that permanent essential nature; and it is also the all-pervading great bliss.

He teaches these in detail in the words beginning "arising": arisen things themselves, like a firebrand wheel and the like, are ultimately unarisen. With these the nature of the enjoyment body is taught.

He teaches luminosity itself in brief in the words beginning "because it is not inert": because its nature is the opposite of inert things. "Not knowable," because its nature is the subsiding of all mental conceptions. "Not visible," because it is not within the range of the eye and the other faculties. Or else the two words apply in order: because it is not apprehended by the ālaya consciousness and the afflicted mind, nor by the six engaging consciousnesses.

Objection: "Is it an entity or a non-entity? And if it is an entity, is it permanent or impermanent? If it is a permanent entity, it would have to possess continuity; and if it is impermanent, earlier and later would not accord." Refuting these, he speaks the words beginning "formless": because no wheel of aspects is apprehended. "Not abiding anywhere" means: because it does not possess a continuity like a staff, in which earlier and later are not destroyed. A permanent thing having form would be permanent by abiding in a collection of entities; and that too is impossible, because entities perish in an instant. Then, if one asks how it is permanent, he answers "because it is unchanging," just as space shows no change.

If one says, "Then it would become a non-entity," he answers in the words beginning "conventional": because it remains for as long as saṃsāra lasts, and because birth occurs conventionally, annihilation is not possible either. Rather, this alone is the primordially established nature of all phenomena; nothing becomes an entity through some other nature.

That is stated in the words beginning "innate": the uncreated nature of phenomena is to be called "the innate." It is, moreover, the nature of supreme joy, which transcends every world. Or else, it is the innate of all phenomena, which has other names such as luminosity. In "and the nature of innate joy," what remains to be supplied is: "the very nature of innate joy becomes clear through the kindness of the guru." This is "self-blessed and self-arisen," because it abides in itself. "Indestructible" means unchanging. "Imperishable," because it is permanent.

If one asks, "How is one to meditate on great bliss, whose nature is not apprehended by conceptual thought? And yet if one does not meditate on it, it will not become directly perceived," he answers in the words beginning "unarisen." That is stated:

When, through the abandoning of arising,
the mind itself is that unarisen,
then, because it rests upon that,
it is as though this reality were realized.

He shows the reason for what was said in the words beginning "wisdom itself": the mind that apprehends all phenomena as without intrinsic nature is wisdom. That itself is meditative concentration, because it abides as one taste with it. That is stated in the words beginning "all phenomena": "that of which, or by which, all phenomena are fully known," likewise; it is in the neuter. This itself is great bliss, the perfect and complete awakening, because it pervades the entire element of space, is common to all, and is uninterrupted. Or else: it is both great and bliss, that is, innate joy. "Of this," meaning that through turning toward it one attains perfect and complete awakening, is the sixth case, referring to that person.

If one asks how the nature of phenomena is pointed out as distinguished from them, he answers in the words beginning "phenomena." "Of phenomena"; or else phenomena themselves are that reality, because the word "phenomenon" also denotes the nature of reality. Engaging with it in that way serves to realize it gradually; in essential nature they are simply undivided. That is stated:

These two truths are joined in union;
they cannot be cut apart and do not become distinct.
They culminate beyond the two conventional designations;
whoever knows them, though bound here, is liberated.

Therefore it is so, because even worldly joy and the rest are pervaded by this alone. By these is expressed what is said:

The cause is to be sealed with the result,
and the result too is to be sealed with the cause.
If one meditates otherwise, accomplishment
will not arise even in ten million eons.

If one asks how these are to be realized, he answers in the words beginning "the holy guru." If one asks where, through the holy guru's instructions, these become clear, the answer is in the words beginning "Cakrasaṃvara." That is stated:

The syllable e, of beautiful form,
adorned in its center by the syllable vaṃ,
the source from which all bliss arises,
is the jewel casket of the buddhas.

What this expresses is: at the time of empowerment, when vajra and lotus are joined, through the holy guru's instructions, by the power of fully arresting the moon, this nature of the fourth empowerment becomes clear. Or else: if one meditates on the syllable e, whose nature is wisdom and which is the nature of the wheels of the emanation and enjoyment bodies at the navel and the throat, joined with the syllable vaṃ, whose nature is method and which is the nature of the wheels of all phenomena and of great bliss at the heart and the crown, innate joy will arise. Or else: when the vaṃ, the mind, enters the e, suchness, it becomes as stated. That is stated:

When mind has entered into emptiness, what brings about equal taste is not the sexual union of the birthplace.

Binding together in this, uniquely and as one, all the phenomena of body, speech and mind and all the aspects such as the aggregates, is the binding (saṃvara). If one asks how these are, he answers in the words beginning "binding." Bliss is the full realization of the sameness of all things, because all conceptual thoughts have subsided; for all phenomena have the nature of mere conceptual thought. That is stated:

Arising and ceasing come about
only through certain conditions.

Or else, from the words "having conferred empowerment, explain reality," the two verses beginning "Cakrasaṃvara" teach the four empowerments, whose nature is the four joys. There, the half verse beginning "Cakrasaṃvara" is the brief teaching. The two lines beginning "body" are the master and secret empowerments. The two lines beginning "binding" are the wisdom-knowledge empowerment. "It is not to be expressed," because it is self-aware. "It cannot be shown," because there is no example for it, either worldly or transcendent. That is stated:

The innate is not expressed by others,
nor is it found anywhere at all;
it is to be known from attending upon the guru
and from one's own merit.

Nor is it comparable to the lotus of the wisdom consort and the like; since there is no other support, that is spoken of merely as an example. That is stated in the Accomplishment of Wisdom:

The impermanent is not great bliss;
great bliss is constant and permanent.
Just as what arises from scratching an itch
is not great bliss.

Or else: there, through the guru's instructions, by the power of arresting the moon, one realizes the reality that cannot be seen; for in the world nothing else of that kind is possible. That is stated:

Therefore whatever bodies have amassed heaps of merit, that is but partial;
I pay homage to those accomplished ones whose minds are unmoving in union with the seal.

If one asks how the earlier and the later statements are not contradictory: they are not, because the earlier disparages only changing bliss. That is stated: "When the moon has passed into the vajra, this again serves no purpose at all." That is not said with regard to unchanging bliss.

That is stated:

Therefore people should rely, with the utmost effort, on unchanging bliss alone;
Having abided in it for long, seize once again the reality born of meditative equipoise.

Alternatively, it is held that the earlier statement serves to turn back clinging to the cause as though it were the result, and the later one serves to teach the cause; so there is no contradiction. For that very reason it is also to be called coemergent (sahaja), because when it is cultivated in this way together with method, by it one attains what transcends the world.

Thus, too, embraced by an action seal (karmamudrā) who takes birth in the form of the desired goddess, or else by a wisdom seal (jñānamudrā), one endowed with the yoga of one's own chosen deity should meditate on that very bliss arising from the example, which is described as complete enjoyment, union, great bliss, absence of intrinsic nature, compassion, uninterruptedness, and unceasingness, as endowed with these seven limbs, and should bring it to perfection as transcending the world. Such is the oral instruction. However, the gurus say that although there is union with the wisdom seal and the like, once meditation has reached its culmination, union with the action seal alone becomes the most excellent and ultimate means. Thus, too:

He bestows the empowerment of the vajra essence;
The supreme drop itself is drawn in.

That came up incidentally. Now what is at hand shall be explained. "Secret" and so on is the fourth, consisting of two lines. "Of all buddhas" means of the aggregates. "Assembly" means having become of one taste. In order to show that all this can be realized only through the kindness of the guru, it is said again: "Self-blessing" and so on.

This is the explanation of the third chapter.

To answer the third question, it says "Then" and so on. "Whatever" means: whatever things there are, having the nature of the stable and the moving, all of them abide as the nature of the four great elements; this is the remainder of the phrase to be supplied. These too are of two kinds, by the division into the external and the internal. Among them, the function and nature common to both are stated: "Earth" and so on. "Among them" means among those. "Earth" means of earth. Or else it is earth itself, namely the characteristics of hardness, solidity and so forth in whatever things there are. By this the function of earth's characteristic, supporting, and its nature, hardness, are also shown. "By fire everything is cooked": by this, fire's nature, heat, and its activity, ripening, are also shown. "Water brings about assimilation": water's nature, wetness, and its activity, holding together, are also shown. "Wind sets things moving together": wind's nature, motion, and its activity, setting in motion, are also shown. "The domain of space": first, its nature, emptiness, and its activity, opening up room, are also shown. That is stated: "Abiding in emptiness, by that it arises." That is, all things come to arise abiding in emptiness; "by that" means by this reason, the giving of room. For that very reason space is not a great element. Rather, because they bear their own characteristics, as elements, only the four, earth and the rest, are the great elements. Thus there is no contradiction in this detailed exposition of the words of the summary.

If one asks whether their characteristics are established by themselves or not, it says "Where" and so on; by this it states that they have the nature of mere imputation, dependent upon one another. Ascertaining the meaning of what was stated: "Grass" and so on. Grass and the like are nothing but the four great elements of the vessel world. "Insentient matter, mere consciousness": the meaning of these words is only twofold, by the division into the insentient and the non-insentient. For that very reason "mere" serves to exclude any other meaning. Alternatively, the meaning of the words is that, since it has the nature of mere luminosity, even insentient matter is consciousness itself. "The six destinies" is connected with the preceding "the four great elements." If one asks how, that being so, the division into sentient beings and the vessel world is made, it says: "Engaging together with consciousness." That is, whatever engages together with consciousness is to be posited as a sentient being. That is stated: "Therefore" and so on. "Therefore" means through the combining of the four great elements. "For all" means that for all beings it becomes a bodily mass; thus the intelligent should understand the things of existence.

If one asks how, since at all times everything has the nature of the four elements, changes such as the death of sentient beings occur, it says "In death and the rest" and so on. "Mover" means "it moves." "In all the limbs and lesser limbs" means that it goes everywhere, into the whole body and all the limbs; the meaning is that when the wind is set moving, then there comes to be no feeling. Drawing the topic together: "Birth" and so on. "The elements go everywhere" means the elements go to every place. Their going everywhere is itself stated: "Gods" and so on. "Not without the elements": the remainder to be supplied is that if they are suppressed by the elements, nothing at all will be born. Showing just that: "Gods" and so on. The meaning is that in the bodies of all the gods too, the world guardians and the rest, they abide together with those guardians. By this it is stated that all of them too will die. Or else: only they are born and die, not the Blessed One, the vajra of mind.

If one asks: it has been stated that these are not different from mind, and that mind at all times remains continuously in a uniform way; how then are there death and birth, characterized by the decline and the arising of the concordance and non-concordance of the elements? It says "Entirely" and so on. Its meaning is this. "Always" means: throughout beginningless saṃsāra, because they do not fully understand the nature as it has been stated, the deluded uphold and cultivate those treatises, the Vedānta and the rest, which teach difference from mind and a nature of permanence, impermanence and so forth; and in the world, too, wrong views arise from them. That is stated by Āryadevapāda:

Seeing the many millions of non-Buddhists (tīrthikas)
Become causes of what is meaningless,
Toward people who long for the Dharma,
In whom would compassion not arise?

If one asks how the apprehended object, set apart by appearing as different from the apprehender, is nonetheless not different from mind, it says "Where, in all" and so on. Because from which, everywhere, all go into all the elements, they are all-pervading. "The element of consciousness abides" means: what has come to abide, all that is born from the earth; what has arisen from the elements is also that very thing. Or else, because in all, all are all-pervading, the element of consciousness and what is born from the earth, that is, the other elements too, come to abide: so the words are connected. For that very reason it says immediately afterward:

Where one abides,
There all four are present.

By these it is stated as follows: because there is no intrinsic nature established by itself, difference is impossible in what has the nature of mere conceptuality, made by one's own mind alone. That itself is stated: "Other than the four elements, the chief": other than the four elements, consciousness alone is chief, and

They are its very emanations:
This is rightly held in all the treatises.

Alternatively, "all" means that in all the later treatises too they hold and meditate on these very ones; therefore the intent is that these alone have become chief. Teaching just these: "In all" and so on. "In all" means the all-pervading ones. Or else "in all" is a qualification of the action as applying to all, and the same holds for the word "all." "Born on the grounds" means born in what has the nature of the nine, and is connected with "they abide in all of them." "Born on the grounds" is a locative plural; so there is no fault, since according to other views of the śrāvakas some matter may possibly occur even in the formless realm.

Summing up the explanation, it says "The four elements" and so on. By these it is explained that only by rightly relying on the four elements should the yogin, by way of mantra, become accustomed to the yoga of the generation and completion stages.

Having set out the common characteristics, it states the special characteristics, the characteristics of the internal elements: "Life" and so on. Because it brings about life and because it moves, life is wind. "Rightly abides" means it is to be understood: the intent is that wind should be understood as the joining of consciousness, having the nature of exhalation and inhalation. "Fire has the characteristic of heat": what has the characteristic of heat is fire, because it produces the nature of radiance. "Water has the nature of nectar": it makes the mortal immortal. Therefore "water has the nature of the deathless," because it makes stable by way of satisfying. "Earth is merely the support": because, like a field, it is the cause of birth, abiding and growth, earth is the support. The meaning is this: in the state of saṃsāra toward objects, and in the transcendent state toward great bliss, these, respectively by impelling, by radiance, by satisfying and by holding, embrace the king of mind, sustain it, and abide. Alternatively, in the casket of the body the king of mind abides by rightly relying on these very ones; and since if any one of them is lacking one dies, they are said to sustain consciousness in a unique way. Space is utterly inseparable from consciousness; rather, it abides by being of one taste with it, and so it is not taught separately. Or else its very giving of room is its sustaining, and that was stated in the context of the common characteristics.

These are stated:

The deity who always abides there
Is the lord of supreme consciousness.

It is a deity because of its very disposition as a deity. Now, to answer the question "How are the five aspects deities?", it says "Its" and so on. "Its" means of consciousness endowed with the five elements. "Enters everywhere" means that all aspects arise from every side. "Deity" means of the deity. "The deities of the five aspects" means both the five aspects and the five deities. Here the five aspects are the five aggregates and the five wisdoms. That is stated: "Form" and so on. "Form" means what appears separately, having the nature of various forms; likewise feeling and the rest. "Mirror" means the mirror wisdom, and likewise the others. "These wisdoms fully abide" means that these, the aggregate of form and the rest, are nothing but the nature of wisdom. Or else the meaning is that, from the word "wisdom," these words "mirror" and the rest stand explicitly and separately; therefore it becomes "mirror wisdom," "wisdom of equality," and so on. The five deities are taught: "Vairocana" and so on. These too are, in order, the purified natures of the aggregate of form and the rest, having the nature of the mirror wisdom and the rest, and chiefly relying on the elements, earth and the rest. These in turn are the natures of the goddesses, Locanā and the rest. To show that the meditator of the generation stage should meditate, through just their purification, by the five manifest awakenings, it says "The five aspects" and so on: "of this realization of complete awakening, in which the five aspects are one," where "of this" refers to the sixth, Vajradhara.

To answer the fifth question, the one about the six objects, it says "Objects" and so on, up to:

Abiding as the supreme of all aspects,
One should realize the purity of objects.

Here the word "objects" also expresses the subject, because objects are dependent on a subject. By these the twelve sense sources are taught. "These" means of these. "Pure" means purification. "Explained" refers to the well-known ones, Rūpavajrā and the rest, and Kṣitigarbha and the rest. "The elements are held to be eighteen": by dividing just these there are the eighteen elements. "From these, the nature of the aggregates": from just these, condensed, comes the nature of the five aggregates. "One should meditate on the supreme unchanging": again, that very one becomes the supreme unchanging, Vajrasattva.

"Buddhahood itself becomes result and cause": that very one becomes the result, characterized as buddhahood itself, and also its cause. If one asks how it becomes so, it says, "One should meditate upon the eye and the rest." The meaning is: having oneself become Vajrasattva, one should meditate, in the sense sources of the eye and the rest, on the forms by which that very Blessed One becomes both the result and its cause. That is stated, in detail: "That very Blessed One, abiding in the sense source of the eye in the form of Kṣitigarbha, purifies the three kinds of form and establishes the vajra sense source of the eye."

Teaching just these, it says "Of the eye" and so on. The eye faculty, its object form, and eye consciousness are the magical display of the Blessed One, the mind Vajrasattva. For the word "eye faculty" also expresses the object of the eye. That itself is stated: "Three" and so on. Likewise the word "object" also indicates the subject, as has just been explained. This order of teaching, too, is for the sake of realizing that object and subject are ultimately indivisible. The meaning is that these three become the support for the state of luminosity, whose meaning is natural purity. "Consciousness" means ear consciousness, and likewise for all four. The word "consciousness" should be understood as "what is born from that particular faculty." "Of sound" means sound. "From a nature without any object of reference" is connected with the preceding "the state of luminosity." "Smelling and odor" means the nose and odor. "Pure, it is held to be suchness": because there is no division between phenomena and the nature of reality. "From purity itself" means because of purity itself. "Body, the tangible, and body consciousness" is the nominative plural. "Mind, phenomena, and mind consciousness" means the mind faculty, the element of phenomena, and mind consciousness. "Purified in the heart itself" means that although it is pervasive, it abides in the heart. "Because of purity itself" is connected with the preceding "the state of luminosity."

Summing up, it says "Six" and so on. "The ground of all" means in the ground of all. "Tathāgata" means Mohavajra and the rest. "Abiding in the samādhi of glorious Heruka" refers to the first.
