"Through the yoga of object and subject": the intent is that when one abides in union through the yoga of the objects, Rūpavajrā and the rest, and the subjects, Mohavajra and the rest, nonconceptual bliss arises. Those very points are stated: "Of the object" and so on. The meaning is that one is not a buddha through emptiness alone, but through being endowed with the supreme of all aspects.

By these it is stated as follows. The meditator of the generation stage, having first visualized the body of Vajrasattva, should firmly ascertain, "This body of the Blessed One himself is of one taste with emptiness." Having made manifest that the faculties, the eye and the rest, and the objects, form and the rest, are without intrinsic nature, through the transformation of seeds and signs in due order, one should meditate on the eye and the rest as Mohavajra and the rest, embraced by Rūpavajrā and the rest. One who abides in the completion stage should meditate, according to the guru's instruction, that the Blessed One, glorious Vajrasattva himself, inseparable from suchness, free from conceptuality, whose nature is the great coemergent, abides pervading all things, stable and moving.

Now, to answer the sixth question, it says "Buddha" and so on, as far as "is the nature." The one Blessed One alone, whose nature is union, is imputed and divided into the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha. He is the Buddha by having the nature of the form body. He is the Dharma by having the nature of the dharmakāya. He is the Saṅgha because that very one abides on the grounds distinguished as the Very Joyful and the rest; for only those who have attained the grounds, the Very Joyful and the rest, are the Saṅgha. These very ones are the three refuges.

That reality is the realities of body, speech and mind: these are the three realities. Emptiness, signlessness and wishlessness are the three gates of liberation. Their purification is the three faces; here they are the faces of the Blessed One. "Three deities" means the Permanent One, Amitābha and Akṣobhya. "Three maṇḍalas" means the wheels of body, speech and mind. "Three yogas" means the supreme king of samādhi, the supreme king of the maṇḍala, and the supreme king of action. "Three paths" means the three paths distinguished as those of the śrāvakas, the pratyekabuddhas and the bodhisattvas. "Three pledges" means deity, mantra and mudrā; or else vajra, bell and deity; or else the three kinds, the moral discipline of restraint and the rest. "Three virtues" means the beginning, the middle and the end. "Three secrets" means the secrets of body, speech and mind. "From the nature of the source of phenomena": because it abides with the nature of the seal of the triangular source of phenomena. "Through the yoga that does not apprehend the three spheres" means through the object of meditation, the meditator and the meditation. "And the nature of the three mantras" means the heart, the near-heart and the garland mantras.

"Outer and inner" and so on: to answer the seventh question, it says "Outer" and so on, up to "well known." Here "outer" is the vessel world, and its purification is the celestial palace. "Inner" is the world of sentient beings, and its purification is the wheel of deities. The purpose is stated: "Outer" and so on. Then, with reference to the wheel of deities, it says "Dharma" and so on. "Visualizing the deities" means meditating on the twenty-four heroes. "As the nature of the dharma realm" is connected with "individually." "From the nature of all aspects, one should fully conceive the goddesses": the goddesses are the nature of the wheel of aspects. Or else they are the natures of the aspects of the thirty-seven factors of awakening.

First, in the form of the deity,
Vajrasattva abides.

The first form of the deity is the chief in the center. The places for arranging the deities of the maṇḍala are stated: "Places" and so on. By "Likewise" and so on, emanation is stated; the remainder to be supplied is: just as the deities dwell within the celestial palace, so, in the manner of union and great bliss, they emanate countless forms and act for the benefit of beings in the ten directions. That is stated: "Countless" and so on. The intent is that the number applies only to those dwelling in the maṇḍala, not to the emanations. It is said that since the number of the emanations cannot be measured, it is inconceivable: "Inconceivable" and so on. If one asks from what these are emanated, it says "Glorious" and so on, which is easy to understand. Or else, "Glorious Heruka in perfect union": the remainder to be supplied is that Cakrasaṃvara and Vārāhī themselves abide in the form of ḍākinīs, and they too are ultimately indivisible. By these a summary is stated. That is stated: "They" and so on. The result is stated: "All of them" and so on.

If one asks how these are to be trained in, it says "Coarse" and so on. It is stated as follows: by holding the entire sequence from the guru's mouth and meditating on it, at the stage when familiarity with one-pointed mind has become excellent, wisdom free of conceptual thought will arise. Or else reality is of two kinds: what the guru teaches, and the way of experiencing it; the result of both of these is the third. That is stated by the master known as Rope:

The wisdom of reality is of two kinds:
Whatever is expressible and inexpressible.

Or else, "The coarse is sound": the coarse has the nature of sound, the nature of the syllables āḥ and hūṃ and so on. "The subtle has the nature of thought": the stages of meditating on it come from the guru's instruction. "Realizing the reality free of thought": through familiarity with that one comes to realize the bliss free of conceptual thought. It is said that, in order to show that the five questions, on the four elements and the rest, have one and the same subject, they are answered in a single chapter. This should be understood likewise in the other chapters.

This is the explanation of the fourth chapter.

With reference to the eighth question, "How are the moon and the sun deities?", it says "Then" and so on. In this chapter what is to be explained is the teaching of all virtue and nonvirtue, which serve as the support of consciousness, such as pleasure and pain, life and death; and the reality of wind, a limb of the stages of vajra recitation. Because that relies on the two channels known as lalanā and rasanā, the characteristics of the two channels are stated first: from "Moon" and so on up to "in due order" is the brief teaching, and "Throat" and so on is the detailed explanation. "Lower mouth": although both of its openings are mouths, because it takes in and expels, the channel at the navel below is its mouth, like a mouth; thus it is the lower mouth. Likewise the upper mouth. "Enters": because consciousness, issuing through the right, grasps an object and immediately, mounted on the wind, enters through lalanā. Those very points are stated: "Of the nose" and so on.

Now, in order to state the topic at hand, the characteristics of the change and non-change of wind, it first states just the arrangement of day and night with respect to outer days and nights: "Day" and so on, which is easy to understand. In order to show the wind of balanced elements moving in sequence, abiding naturally in a single day and night that consists of eight watches, it says "Shifts" and so on, which is easy to understand.

If one asks: in a day and night sixteen shifts take place, and the wind moves through whichever nostril it began to move through from sunrise on one day, and through that very one it moves at all times when abiding naturally; how then, on the morning of the fourth day, does the wind move through the other nostril? It should be said: here, in one day and night, ninety-six maṇḍalas shift. When it shifts from one maṇḍala to another, in the way that will be explained, the force known as the daughters of Māra consumes half a breath each time; so forty-eight breaths of the ninety-six maṇḍalas are consumed, and in three days one hundred and forty-four breaths are cut off. Likewise, at the time when night and day are equal, when it shifts from one nostril to the other, from the twenty-five breaths and slightly less than three quarters of a breath that move in both nostrils, the movement of one nostril, amounting to one thousand three hundred and fifty, is cut off. So it went: on the morning of the fourth day it moves through the other nostril. This is a digression.

The topic at hand shall be stated. If one asks how the daṇḍa is laid out in this arrangement, it says "Wind" and so on. Here, first, showing the measure of the daytime daṇḍa at the sun's transit into Capricorn (Makara), it says "Breaths" and so on. "Each with its quarter" means that the three breaths too each have a quarter. Therefore fifty palas, three breaths and three quarters of a breath make one daṇḍa of the day at the beginning of the northern course. In terms of the total of breaths, it is three hundred and four breaths less one quarter. The night daṇḍa is sixty-one palas plus five breaths and a quarter; in terms of the total of breaths, it is three hundred and seventy-one breaths and a quarter. By such a measure of the daṇḍa, the night is thirty-two. Then, from the sun's northern course, immediately each day the daytime daṇḍa increases and the nighttime daṇḍa decreases.

If one asks how much it increases and decreases each month, and how much each day, it says "By breaths" and so on. In one month, one daṇḍa gains eleven breaths and one quarter. Therefore three hundred and fifteen breaths make the daytime daṇḍa at the transit into Aquarius (Kumbha), and at night three hundred and sixty breaths make one daṇḍa.

So it goes at each transit, as far as the southern course. If one asks how much it increases and decreases each day so as to amount to eleven breaths and a quarter, it says "Of breaths" and so on; this is connected with the preceding: "a third of a breath plus a twenty-fourth part increases."

Suppose someone objects: since each daṇḍa increases by a third of a breath plus a twenty-fourth part, over just the thirty-two daṇḍas it comes to two palas; yet in the outer treatises and in other tantras such as the Vajraḍāka it is held that each day increases by four palas. Moreover, with the measure of a daṇḍa consisting of sixty palas, the day of the northern course has twenty-four daṇḍas, which in number of breaths is eight thousand six hundred and forty breaths; whereas by the system of this tantra the total of breaths in the day of the northern course is nine thousand seven hundred, so that here there are one thousand one hundred and ten more, a very great discrepancy. To this it should be said: since the two have different subjects, there is no such fault. For this tantra gives the inner arrangement, since it states without a break:

Through the sixteen limbs of transit,
The wind changes in one day and night;
In the openings of the nostrils, always,
It truly moves for half a watch each.

Here, in the equal night and day, in the wheel of emanation, which consists of sixty-four channels, by the arrangement in which three hundred and thirty-seven and a half breaths move in each channel, sixty-four channels are traversed; hence there are sixty-four daṇḍas. Likewise in the great bliss wheel at the crown, which consists of thirty-two channels, by the arrangement in which twice that, six hundred and seventy-five breaths, move in each channel, thirty-two channels are traversed; hence there are thirty-two water-clock periods (ghaṭikās). Likewise in the wheel of enjoyment, which consists of sixteen channels, by the arrangement in which twice that, one thousand three hundred and fifty breaths, move in each channel, there are sixteen half-watches. Likewise in the wheel of Dharma, which consists of eight channels, two thousand seven hundred breaths move in each channel, and there are eight watches. So too it says in the King of the Two Examinations: "Moon and sun, āli and kāli, the sixteen transits, the sixty-four daṇḍas, the thirty-two ghaṭikās and the eight watches."

In the Vajraḍāka and the rest, because the work of calculation is primary, the arrangement is the outer one; therefore there is no contradiction with this. Or else this is an arrangement common to outer and inner. Thus too, "Sunrise" and so on: from the division of the day into four watches and the night into four watches come the eight watches of a day and night, the sixteen half-watches, the thirty-two ghaṭikās by grouping the quarter-watch periods, and the sixty-four daṇḍas by arranging their measure as daṇḍas. This is the arrangement of the outer alone. For the measure of the daṇḍa is set according to one's own wish; thus the astronomer Bhasata made a hundred daṇḍas in one day and night. Here the arrangement by the sixty-four daṇḍas is the inner one and the common one, just as has been explained. For that reason too this one is beautiful.

Thus there are four great transits: the two equal ones, the northern course and the southern course. Among these, because it is primary and because the arrangement is easy, the arrangement of the equal daṇḍa is stated, with the characteristics of the inner and outer breaths and another enumeration of palas, preceded by the characteristic of the life-breaths: "Life-breaths" and so on. Whatever breaths belong to fifty life-breaths: the numbers of breaths laid out in order by them; and on top of that, three, which have become what they are by first placing a zero; "by those, with quarters endowed with limbs," that is, thirty of its quarters; "truly endowed with those quarters, these make the equal daṇḍa," which is the remainder to be supplied. By these, three hundred and thirty-seven and a half is the equal daṇḍa, as before.

In order to distinguish the day and night daṇḍas of the northern and southern courses, it says "Of the northern course" and so on. By forty-five of these palas is the daytime daṇḍa of the northern course, which in total of breaths comes to two hundred and seventy. The night daṇḍa is sixty-seven and a half palas, which in total of breaths is four hundred and five. As is the daytime daṇḍa of the northern course, so is the night daṇḍa of the southern course.

If one asks how these change, "Daṇḍa by daṇḍa" is the brief teaching; its detailed explanation is given: "By breaths" and so on. "Eleven with a quarter, and eleven with a quarter": by these they increase and decrease at each transit. These are stated: from the transit into Libra (Tulā) and onward for as long as the northern course lasts, and from the transit into Aries (Meṣa) and onward for as long as the southern course lasts, over three transits, at each transit each daṇḍa gains or loses twenty-two and a half breaths, increasing and decreasing, and decreasing and increasing, respectively, according to the division into night and day; and through the increase and decrease of sixty-seven and a half breaths it becomes just the daṇḍa as stated.

If one asks how much one night daṇḍa increases and one day daṇḍa decreases each day, so as to amount to twenty-two and a half breaths in a month, it says "Of the breath" and so on. "A third of a breath" means three portions; by that.

If one asks by how much it increases and decreases through the sixty-two daṇḍas, it says "That quarter" and so on. The word "quarter" is meant to be carried over together with "the summed monthly increase." "The summed monthly increase itself, with a sixth part of a quarter" means: with a quarter of a pala, which consists of six quarter-breaths, that is, together with a breath and a half. By these it is explained that, through the thirty-two daṇḍas, night and day each increase and decrease by twenty-four breaths.

Having shown the state in which things remain normal, it also states the reversal and its result: the passage beginning "The watch is half" is easy to understand. "Changing" means this: if the breath flows for six days, death comes after three years less twenty-four days. If it flows for seven days, after three years less twice that. If it flows for eight days, after three years less three times twenty-four. If it flows for nine days, after three years less four times twenty-four; that is, one dies at the very moment at which the breath shifts to the other nostril. In the same way, elsewhere too, this is the meaning of the word "changing."

Having indicated untimely and timely death, it then shows how to cheat it, saying "According to the rite" and so on. "Channel" means the lalanā and the rasanā as well; these are the aids for cheating death. The rite of cheating death will be explained below.

To show the breaths of day and night that have been explained, it says "Exceeding by six hundred" and so on.

Now, further, it states the aids by which the yogin of wind accomplishes the rites of pacifying and so on: "Mahendra" and so on. That is, the rites of enriching and the like are performed in the Mahendra maṇḍala. Likewise, "Through water, aims come about perfectly" means that the desired results, such as pacifying, are accomplished. "Hunger and thirst" means that when the wind flows in the fire maṇḍala, fierce rites are accomplished. To say that the particular forms of fierce rite, such as expulsion, are accomplished from wind, it says "From wind" and so on. "Mahendra" means in Mahendra. "Gain" means for the gain of goods. Obtaining it is full obtaining; here the first case is used in the sense of the sixth. "One who gathers goes" is the remainder to be supplied. "Water-born" and so on also refer to spectacles.

Therefore, having first said "the supreme yoga, the chief, the reality of wind," if one asks what kind of reality of wind is chief, it says "As in the speech of Vajrasattva." This means that just as the Blessed One Vajrasattva taught it, so it is supreme. Or else the intent is this: just as the speech of Vajrasattva, being undeceiving in every respect, has become supremely authoritative, so too the reality of wind that has been explained and will be explained is taught.

Concerning what is to be known, since it was asked that when questioned by another the yogin should know what is positive and what is negative, it says "Wind" and so on, which is easy to understand.

Having shown the divisions of wind that change according to circumstance, to show the reality of wind in its natural state, it says "Life" and so on. "Moves variously" means that within a single maṇḍala they are of the nature of five rays, the five Tathāgatas. "From the right" means from the right side of a single nostril alone, and likewise from the left side. "From both" means from the middle part between the two. "Lower" means touching straight along the bottom and the sides. "Moving slowly" means going cool and soft, not fast. "The vajra lord" is Akṣobhya. "Accompanies the whole body" because, being of the nature of the space maṇḍala, it is all-pervading.

These are the five winds, the life wind and the others. Of them, the upward-moving wind, whose nature is Amitābha, resides in the throat. The equalizing wind, whose nature is Amoghasiddhi, resides in the navel. The downward-clearing wind, whose nature is Ratnasambhava, resides in the secret place. The life wind, whose nature is Akṣobhya, resides in the heart. The pervading wind has the nature of Vairocana, and its location has already been stated. Although it would have been fitting to present Amoghasiddhi first, since he is the father of Tārā, who is of the nature of the wind element, Amitābha was presented first, because the stage of vajra recitation pertains to vajra speech.

If one asks why the movement of the space maṇḍala, known as the pervading wind, was not stated, it is because it does not occur on its own. If one asks how, in that case, its movement in the maṇḍalas is established, it is by its simply following the four maṇḍalas. Thus, in the movement of a single maṇḍala, which consists of two hundred and a quarter, immediately after every thirty-one breaths one breath flows in the channel called the daughters of Māra, and the remaining half breath flows as well; so through the seven rounds, seven and a half breaths are established as the movement of the pervading wind. Therefore the principal movement belongs to the four maṇḍalas alone; that of space simply follows them and is not separate.

If one asks how many maṇḍalas flow in which nostril, and in which nostril a maṇḍala flows first, it should be said: as many breaths as there are in one maṇḍala when night and day are equal, so many are the palas, also called prāṇas, in one nostril. Therefore "the maṇḍalas come to flow" refers to the sequence in which the maṇḍalas flow according to the division of the nostrils. From the words

Space and the rest are always on the left;
on the right are always earth and the rest,

it is to be understood through the sequence of dissolving and arising.

"Through the signs of death" means that one should strive to cheat death. It gives three methods of cheating, according to the division into dull, middling and sharp faculties: "The wind" and so on, which is easy to understand.

Then, for when one cannot cheat death even by the yoga of meditation, it teaches transference of consciousness, from "of the heart" up to "attains the state." The meaning is this: preceded by deity yoga, one should fix the mind on a white syllable hūṃ resting on a moon at the center of a lotus in the heart, and should meditate on the upper and lower winds as joined in union.

"When it is somewhat stable there, one is not bound by objects and the rest" means that objects and the rest do not bind the yogin, because the mind does not stray toward them; and those who are perfectly stable attain the stable state. If perfect stability has not been attained, then at that time one should perform transference by this very practice, which will be made clear in the nineteenth chapter. Although this chapter shares a single subject with the chapter to be explained immediately after it, this chapter is distinct because it mainly teaches the signs of the normal state, of the abnormal state, of timely and untimely death, and of cheating death.

This concludes the explanation of the fifth chapter.

To answer the ninth question, "How do those five paths come about?", it teaches the reality of wind already explained, as distinguished by its being of the nature of the five goddesses who are the five maṇḍalas: "From this" and so on. Earlier it was taught as the five Tathāgatas, of the nature of the five rays; restating it here is no fault. For that very reason, "the five paths" means that the five elements are of the nature of the paths of the rites of pacifying and so on. To state these, it says "Self and other" and so on. "Excellent" means perfect. "Of the maṇḍala" means in the maṇḍala. "In the movement" means "of the wind in question."

It says this: what was stated, that at the time of shifting from one maṇḍala to another, half a breath is cut off in the channel called the daughters of Māra, is the yogin's entry into the central channel. With this very point in mind, the glorious Guhyasamāja says:

By the rite in the manner of knowledge,
through the thorough practice of the four sessions.

For that very reason the masters say that what for others is the daughters of Māra is, for the yogin, the Deathless Daughter. If one asks whether the daughters of Māra are the avadhūtī, they are not; rather, they rest entirely on the avadhūtī. Thus it is said:

Trivṛttā, Kāminī, Gehā,
Caṇḍikā, and the Deathless Daughter.

These five, in the wheel of Dharma, are in sequence of the nature of the elements, earth and the rest. Therefore one should take this as indicating the shift from one maṇḍala to another. The rest is easy to understand.

Here one should understand that the reality of mantra is also indicated. Or else: what was taught in the fifth chapter by the three verses beginning "of the heart," here is the reality of wind alone. Hence too, the cause of vajra recitation is the reality of mantra, and its cause is the reality of wind; this is the order of explanation in which the cause precedes the result. Therefore it will be explained that the yogin of the initial deity yoga, after first examining the reality of wind, should also train in vajra recitation through the stages of the reality of mantra.

This concludes the explanation of the sixth chapter.

To answer the fourth question, "What is the nature of the body?" and so on, it says "Then" and so on. Since the channels are many, it first states their number: "seventy-two." These are the seventy-two thousand common channels. Among them, as the principal mounts of wind and supports of consciousness, in the four wheels called those of Dharma, enjoyment, great bliss and emanation, there are respectively eight, sixteen, thirty-two and sixty-four; these hundred and twenty are the principal ones. Among these, the twenty-four places, the crown of the head and the rest, are principal, since they are the causes of the growth and maintenance of the twenty-four bodily constituents, such as the nails and teeth. Among all of these, three are principal, since they are what gathers them all together. And among those, the central channel is principal, since it is the cause that produces great bliss. Thus the passage from "Channel" up to "where the nasal mucus flows" is, in its meaning, the generation stage, as is well known from the commentary on the Abhisamaya of Lūyipa. Some say that in the constituents of the twenty-four channels one should meditate on the twenty-four syllables beginning pū ja, resting on seats of suns, together with crescent moon and drop.

Having shown which channels are principal and which are not, their number, places and functions, and also the nature of the body, it then shows what the bodily mass is, saying "Their assembly" and so on. "Their" means of those. The mass of the assembly, fully arisen in the form of deities, is the assembly of the channels of the places, the crown of the head and the rest, and these are, in order, the goddesses Pracaṇḍā and the others. For that very reason it says "Form" and so on. Form is natural form. "Mass" means the body. What is beyond the mass is luminosity, and that is the deity. To sum these up, it says "Therefore" and so on.

It means this: the meditator of the generation stage, realizing the inner self as pure, meditates according to the rite on the wheel of thirty-seven deities within the outer celestial palace. Then, by the sequence "the beings, drawn in by the joyous sound of the wisdom being, into the charnel ground; that into the samaya wheel," and so on, he makes them non-appearing. He abides in the nature of suchness, free of conceptual thought, and again and again rises from it and enters into it. Having attained one taste with it, at the culmination he becomes free of habitual tendencies and attains the wisdom in which rising and entering are not two.

Or else: these very goddesses, Pracaṇḍā and the rest, meditated on within the celestial palace, are the assembly; and "mass" means the deity nature fully arisen, gathered together at the crown of the head and the other places. For that very reason it says "beyond form," because, once the aspects of arms and faces have been entirely abandoned, they are realized as being of the nature of the channels. "Mass" means the body.

The meaning is this: at first these goddesses abide in the celestial palace and accomplish the aims of beings by emanating and withdrawing. Then the yogin who realizes them in the body as being of the nature of the channels, free of conceptual thought, produces the accomplishment of the great seal (mahāmudrā), which is of the nature of great bliss of one taste with suchness. For that very reason, "that which is clear" means those deities. To sum them up, it says "Therefore" and so on. Stating the result, it says "Whose" and so on; "will attain" means causes one to attain.

Or else, "Fully arisen from their assembly" means that, by the sequence of fully arising from their assembly, it arises from Jālandhara through the brow, the throat and the heart, up to the tip of the vajra jewel. The mass is what has become one, because the one whose nature is the deity revels in great bliss from all the channels. "Beyond form" means beyond any object of focus, because it does not change from that state. "Mass" means that, having pervaded the whole body, it reaches its own place. "Beyond the mass" means that the deity is just that, because great bliss of one taste with emptiness has been attained. "Therefore" means by the nature that has come about. "Through inconceivable union" means through being free of conceptual thought. "All-pervading, of the nature of suchness": supply "one becomes a body of the nature of emptiness that pervades everything."

Stating its activity, it says "Who" and so on. "Its self" means having attained the nature of the self of supreme bliss. "Will attain": supply "causes sentient beings to attain."

Or else, it is also from the particulars stated here and from other tantras: through any single practice whatever, one abides in the state of luminosity, and by that very nature one is one with glorious Heruka and the others. Having thereby attained the nature of innate great bliss of one taste with suchness, the yogin will truly attain buddhahood, entirely without conceptual thought, which is like a jewel of many forms, because it is the cause of showing to all who are to be trained the forms they desire, and like a wish-fulfilling jewel, because it completely fulfills the wishes of beings. Since the ways of the path are infinite, there is no inconsistency at all; this is the intent of the Blessed One. As it is said:

Those skilled in method should not disparage it in the least;
the perfect buddhas have taught measureless methods for beings.

This concludes the explanation of the seventh chapter.

To answer the fourteenth question, "Samaya" and so on, it says "Then" and so on. Samaya is what the yogin must necessarily practice and must not transgress. It is of two kinds: what is to be guarded and what is to be consumed. Because of the many divisions of their types, it is stated in the plural, "the samayas." Stating the result, it says "By which" and so on.

Then, with regard to the samaya to be consumed, it states in order the determination of the place, of those to be worshipped, of the rite of worship, and of the requisites. From "One's own house" and so on up to "because one wishes the unsurpassed fruit" is the determination of the place. From "Yogin" and so on up to "highly praised" is the determination of those to be worshipped. From "Having invited" and so on up to "should be consecrated" is the determination of the rite of worship. The half verse beginning "Mead" is the determination of the requisites. The rest is also the rite itself.

There, "Field-born, mantra-born and place-born" means that those born from the field and from mantra are the field-born and the mantra-born, and likewise the place-born. Field and place are given as examples, so the secondary fields are included as well. "Mantra-born" means those made fortunate through the stages of empowerment and the rest, who are devoted to meditative concentration, mantra recitation and the like. "All the deities should be invited" means that all should be invited in the form of deities. "And the master is a monk": supply "one should make a monk the foremost master." If one asks how the master can be a monk, it says "Some" and so on. There is no contradiction in those who abide on the path through the stages of the way of secret mantra being masters; they remain monks in order to care for people who have no special faith in the profound Dharma. As the Vajramālā says:

Delighting inwardly in the Guhyasamāja,
outwardly they practice the conduct of śrāvakas.

And likewise elsewhere too:

Relying on secret discipline,
one accomplishes the unsurpassed state.

"Some, qualities" means that if someone is endowed with qualities such as the higher perceptions, then even one other than a monk may be made the principal master. "Relying on enjoyments" means making use of the goods of a stupa. Some read "conduct fallen to an extreme." In that case, turning aside from the conduct of samaya to something else is falling to an extreme, because the vows of the śrāvakas and others abide in a single extreme. "Enjoyment" means attachment that abides exclusively in the five qualities of sense pleasure. Or else it means abiding exclusively in eating, that is, never being separated from eating.

"Always with regard for sentient beings" means always holding sentient beings dear, with compassion and kindness. "Excellent" means supreme: the mudrās of the wheel and the rest. "The torma should be examined by the master" means that the master should examine the torma by the rite that will be explained. "At first, from the whole of samaya conduct, together with the hook" means that at the very beginning one sprinkles all the samaya requisites with pills of the five nectars and the five lamps, prepared exactly according to the rite, places them in the position of the wheel, and the master should then consecrate them.

The condensed meaning here is this. In a well-hidden place, one stations gatekeepers to prevent the entry of those who do not hold the samaya. In a house or other thoroughly secluded spot, one arranges seats and the like, and sprinkles the samaya requisites with the samaya pills to purify them. Then, with the master leading, one washes the feet of all the members of the maṇḍala with fragrant water and seats them in order of seniority according to the sequence of their empowerments. After the argha and other offerings have been presented, the master purifies the arranged samaya requisites exactly according to the rite, and the assembly should be satisfied with the offering placed in the hand, preceded by outer offerings such as flowers. The substance that clings to the hand is regarded as like the remainder from the deity's mouth. The karmavajra sprinkles all the food, drink and the rest to purify them, and the master should recite the three syllables.

Then one takes a little of the first portion of each food and the rest, pours it into a large vessel and places it in the middle of the assembly, and first one should offer the great torma adorned with parasols, banners and the like. After that, following it, the karmavajra holds in the right hand the individual vessels, each with three pills of the five nectars and the five lamps, and with the left hand displays the mudrā of the three-flag. With this verse:

Toward the things you see, which are pure,
cast off the thought that they are not;
brahmins, dogs and outcastes,
partake together as one,

he should offer to each of them individually, beginning with the master. Those who receive it should also display that mudrā and accept it with this verse:

The Dharma of the Sugata is priceless,
free from the stain of desire,
having abandoned grasped and grasper;
to that itself I bow with reverence.

Food and the rest should likewise be enjoyed in this way.

Then, reciting the two verses beginning "There, the places and the near places," one should praise the wheel of deities of the chief of the wheel. After that, the patron should offer a maṇḍala, and with palms joined, after praise and homage, should say "As is pleasing." Then, with the verses beginning "Equal to existence," one should make supplication on the patron's behalf to the entire wheel together with the goddesses. Since in the way of the yogin they are the principal ones, one should make supplication to them: the three verses beginning "Yogin," "The syllable e," and "Glorious" are homage to the chief and the rest. The three verses beginning "Then," "Goddess," and "Birth" are homage to the chief lady. The three verses beginning "As many," "Glorious Heruka," and "Nonconceptual" are the praise of the wheel.

In these, the yoga of inseparable emptiness and compassion is the nectar. It is one, that is, nondual; "pure mind" means one whose mind is purified by drinking its taste. "Pure body" means that, by surpassingly traversing the outer and inner places and the rest, the body is stainless. "Seat of glory" means the vajra place, the pistil of the wheel of great bliss. "Great speech" means the scripture. "By whom" means by those who; the thought is "the teacher of the root tantra of the Blessed Lady." The word "tantra" can also mean the one who makes that tantra, as in "making one's own tantra." "Going to seven" means the seven-goer, that is, the sun, because it moves free of the darkness of not knowing reality. "Garland of goddesses" means the assembly of goddesses, of the nature of the wheel of channels, manifest in bodily form; there is no other meaning. Since that is the cause of perfect bliss, it is like a jewel: "whose jewel" it is. "Hero" means through the twenty-four constituents. "Lord abiding in the wheel" means abiding in the wheel of emanation and, as the chief, having become a protector because able to protect all sentient beings. Or else, "abiding in the wheel" means abiding in the wheels of Dharma and the rest, and "protector" means the one whose protector he is. "Innate, stainless" is because it blazes intensely like a flame of its own nature. The syllable yaṃ is restrictive. For that very reason, "the heart of the Cakrasaṃvara yoga" means having become the essence of the yoga of Cakrasaṃvara. "Of this e shape" means the shape of the syllable e. It is the place of the seminal fluid that has become the essence; "the heart of the lotus" means in the support of the lotus of emanation and the rest. "Transferring and being born, the self of all" means pervading the whole body. "Omniscient" is because of being of the nature of Vajrasattva. "The abode of the utterly pure buddha" means that, being Akṣobhya's seal, the self of utterly pure wisdom is the buddha Akṣobhya. "The abode of the goddesses": the goddesses are the channels, of the nature of Pracaṇḍā and the rest; it is the one whose abode they are. "Stainless, innate" is because of the beauty of the jasmine, which is of stainless nature. Glory is supramundane perfection; one who has it is glorious, and that is why. Vajra is indivisible wisdom. ḍā is in the sense of going in the sky. akana: aka is in the sense of moving crookedly. The vajra of indivisible wisdom goes in the sky and, radiating to the ten directions, also moves crookedly: the one of whom this is true. Therefore "whose nature is aki" is said. Revolving in the wheel of those ḍākinīs is the way of the one whose way it is. "Cutting the bonds of conceptualization": the nature of the grasped and the rest is the bond of conceptualization; "cutting" means those by whom it has been abandoned.

To say that, because of the yoga of glorious Heruka, the great hero, the deities of the maṇḍala are also just that, it says "Glorious Heruka" and so on. "Nonconceptual" means free of superimposition. "Correct conception" means the activity of the mind: the one whose activity it is.

For that very reason, "not abiding" means one whose mind does not focus on anything at all. Since there is neither mindfulness nor mental engagement, "Not engaging the mind, without mindfulness" is a form of address. "Holder of the supreme body" means possessing an unsurpassed body. "Having the form of the three realms" means being of the nature of the three realms. "By the threes" refers to the three verses, spoken with the yogin, the yoga and the result, and body, speech and mind in view, either because each shares the same character, or else because they are taught as one.

"Happily and with joyful mind" means with joy in partaking of intoxicating drink (madana) and the rest in such a way that the bliss does not decline. "kili kili" is a vajra song. Showing these, it says "Various" and so on. "With mudrā and mantra" and the rest means that the dances and songs are adorned with the nature of mudrā and mantra. "With the lines on places and signs" means with the vajra lines that enumerate the places and the rest. "Chief of the assembly" means the leader of the wheel. "To the patron's virtuous intention" means taking it as "of this virtuous intention of the patron." "The aspect of the elaborated maṇḍala" means emanating and withdrawing just as the aspect of the maṇḍala is. "Gathering the leftover torma, give it to the unruly bhūtas" means that at the end one gathers up the torma for the wrathful ones and the bhūtas.

It says this. All of them, led by the chief of the assembly, enjoy the five sense pleasures only as pleases them, and being satisfied, they put the patron's virtuous mind first and praise his virtue. Then they fully withdraw the wheel of deities that had been emanated to bring about, by its emanation, the fulfilment of his wishes such as pacifying. Stretching both hands upward, joining the two thumbs pointing downward, joining the tips of the two index fingers and fully stretching the rest, one places this mudrā in front of the mouth, fills the mouth with liquor, and gives the leftover torma with the offering syllable, preceded by reciting the three syllables. Then the patron also offers the fee, betel and the like, and having first pleased them, pays homage, and they should take their leave.

This concludes the explanation of the eighth chapter.

To answer the four questions beginning with the fifteenth, from "Please explain the chomā" up to "will see the signs," it says "Then" and so on. There, from "Then" up to "Who" teaches the code signs. From "Ḍākinī" up to "equal to all buddhas" teaches the outer and inner places of the self and the rest. From "The place is the ground, the Very Joyful" up to "humans" is the teaching on these. From "Circling" up to "is born" teaches the signs.

"Of that left hand" means the left hand; "ni" is for restriction. "Yoga" means the yoginī; for "accomplishment quickly arises," supply "for the yogin." Or else the meaning is that the yogin who recognizes this will quickly gain accomplishment. It says this: whether a yogin of mantra recitation and supreme meditative concentration stays in one place or travels around the places and the rest, the yoginīs reveal themselves to him through code signs in order to take him into their care. When the yogin sees their signs, he too should answer with a sign; this pleases them, and they will bring him true benefit and accomplishment. Here, the yoginīs make their bodily signs with the left, and the yogin answers with the right.

"One finger" means the index finger. "Who" means the question "Have you come well?" "With two" means the answering sign made with the index and middle fingers. "If one presses the left thumb": supply "with the index and middle fingers"; to this, the answering mudrā is made in the same way with the right. Some read "if one joins the left fingers"; in that case it means joining the index and middle fingers. "One-sided" means the index finger; some say the thumb. "Showing the ring finger" means touching with it, likewise at the throat. "Examining silk" is because, holding a fist with the index and middle fingers stretched out and joined, one holds it at the forehead. "Trident" is because the thumb presses the little finger and the remaining three fingers are stretched apart from it. "Earth" means the earth; some say "teeth." "Wheel" comes from joining the tips of the thumb and index finger. "Crown" is because one touches the uṣṇīṣa. "One should display play there" means that one should dance with both hands in a joyful manner.

To say that one should rely on them after recognizing them from their conduct of body, speech and mind as well, it says "With the left" and so on. "With the left" means setting out first with the left foot. "Ḍākinī" is in the first case. "From the left" stands for "of one born." "She who speaks much with the left hand" means one whose manner is to raise the left hand and speak much. Some read "Turning to the left, she makes a sound." "She who speaks joyfully to women" means she speaks to women in melody. "By that she should be known as holding the samaya" means that she is a samaya holder. "The supreme aim of women" means that one should rely on the samayas for their sake. "By the seeds of the families" means by the seeds of Vairocana and the rest. "The activity of the family" means the activity of whatever family one was born into. "Recites her own vidyā" means the mantra of her own family. "Always truly asks about the vidyā" means she asks about one's own mantra. "Delights in asking in her own house": supply "having worshipped." "The ḍākinī truly arisen from the family" means truly arisen from the family of Vajravārāhī. "In the land" and the rest are easy to understand.

"That mass is its nature" means this. There are the twenty-four lands, Pūllira and the rest, gathered into the ten, the pīṭhas and the rest; there are the goddesses of those places, Pracaṇḍā and the rest; and there are the heroes, Khaṇḍakapāla and the rest. The single whole into which all of these are truly gathered has as its nature the body of the yogin. For that very reason it says "all the buddhas" and so on. The intent is that a yogin of sharp faculties should generate all of this within his own body. If one has no capacity within, one should simply not go to the outer pīṭhas and the rest. As it is said in the Abhidhānottara:

By journeying outside, yogins
exhaust their elements.
Therefore set the outer firmly aside
and abide in the play of yoga.
If that reality is absent,
there is no error in it at all.
Or else, if it is endowed with that reality,
there is no error in it at all.

Or else: "Once one has gained capacity in inner recitation, meditation and the like, and has obtained a vision of the goddesses, one should visit the outer pīṭhas and the rest." That which becomes the cause of practicing the conduct in the outer kṣetras and the rest is what is well known as the inner. That is the meaning.

For that very reason it will be explained:

If able, circling the places,
one should truly worship the outer yoginīs.

The earlier statement, "the maturing of the merit of disciples of various kinds," is not in contradiction with this. "Circling" means through the pīṭhas and the rest. "Signs" means the conduct of the yoginīs. That is what it states with "various" and so on: some of them approach the yogin in the forms of tigers and the like, in the forms of yakṣas and the like, and in the forms of wrathful ones and the like, and they also laugh terrifying laughs such as "ha ha." The yogin should not be afraid of that. If one asks what then, it says "oneself pre-eminent" and so on. The meaning is that, visualizing oneself in the form of glorious Cakrasaṃvara embraced by Vajravārāhī, one should make the sound of the syllable hūṃ. That is what it states with "lion" and so on. "Seeing and touching" is said by way of illustration: it should be understood to apply also to hearing words and the like.

This concludes the explanation of the ninth chapter.

To answer the nineteenth question, "How are your twelve activities," it says "Then" and so on. The twelve activities are: pacifying obstructors and the like, pacifying fevers and the like, increasing wealth, increasing the body, bringing under control, summoning by power, immobilizing, making mute, killing, dividing, expelling, and driving mad. Because all activities are included within pacifying, pacifying alone is taught first.

Here is the oral instruction for it. For the pacifying activity, with a peaceful mind, preceded by the giving of torma, one is endowed with the yoga of white, two-armed Cakrasaṃvara facing east. "With saffron and sandalwood mixed" applies to pacifying for a man; for pacifying for a woman, use sandalwood mixed with gīvaṃ. In the bright fortnight, on silk or the like, or on clean cotton cloth, upon a moon maṇḍala, draw "two six-spoked wheels." Some say this means two wheels made of two triangles, like the eight-spoked wheel and the others. Draw them encircled by a vajra garland. On the six spokes, beginning with the east, write in order the six syllables beginning with oṃ, their heads facing outward: on one wheel running clockwise, and on the second running counterclockwise. In the hub write phaṭ svāhā so-and-so śāntiṃ kuru svāhā; for pacifying for a woman, namaḥ Devadatta śāntiṃ kuru namaḥ.

Then place the two face to face, enclose them both between two new bowls, and wind them round with white thread. Set them on a maṇḍala made of sandalwood and the like, and, after first drawing in the wisdom being according to the rite, worship them with white flowers and so on. Then, focusing on the wheel, recite the seven-syllable mantra a thousand times or so. In front, between the wheel and oneself, see the person for whom the rite is done, seated on a moon. From the seed syllable in one's heart emerges white Khaṇḍarohā, holding a crystal vase filled with the water of the nectar of wisdom. Reciting the mantra oṃ sarba tathāgatābhiṣeka samaya śrīye hūṃ with the intention of conferring empowerment, she washes that person with streams of the nectar of wisdom. One should imagine that misdeeds and the like leave through the anus in the form of something black, and that the person becomes pure, like a figure of crystal. Then again, imagine the person illumined by the light rays of the seven-syllable mantra as one recites it, and recite the mantra with a peaceful mind. Do this in three sessions, until the result is accomplished. When the result has been accomplished, remove the bowls and place the wheel on the person's neck or elsewhere, in the manner of a vidyā. Or else, once the wisdom wheel has departed, consign it to water or the like.

The pacifying of fevers and the like is taught with "fever" and so on. Here a yogin endowed with capacity visualizes that very wheel, as described, in his left hand. With the other, the right hand, following the rite just described, he takes a bundle of dūrvā grass, peacock feathers and kuśa grass, or something else, dips it in water, and sprinkles the one afflicted by fever, poison and the like. Visualizing that person seated on a moon, he sits close by facing east and sets three vessels, filled with torma, water and fire, near the person's feet. With the thought that poison and the like are burned there, he inserts the name into so-and-so nirjvalaraṃ nirvaśaṃ kuru svāhā and, with his hands, strokes the person from the crown of the head down to the soles of the feet. One should imagine that the white light rays of the seed syllable in one's heart draw out the fever and the rest in just that way and cast them into the vessels.

Now the wheel of increase is taught with "saffron" and so on. "In two bowls" means inside the two bowls.

The condensed meaning of this is as follows. Facing north, preceded by the giving of torma, endowed with the yoga of yellow, two-armed Cakrasaṃvara, and with a mind set on increase, one draws inside the bowl, on the back of an elephant, two six-spoked wheels. On one of them, running clockwise, the six syllables go on the six spokes; in the hub goes phaṭ bauṣaṭ so-and-so puṣṭiṃ kuru bauṣaṭ svāhā. In the same way, on the other, running counterclockwise, one draws the seven-syllable mantra. The two are placed face to face, wound round with yellow thread, and placed within the wisdom wheel; then they are set in a vessel filled with butter or with honey and worshipped with yellow flowers and the like. Reciting the mantra as before, one imagines, in front between the wheel and oneself, the person for whom the rite is done, seated on a yellow moon maṇḍala. From the seed syllable in one's heart emerges yellow Khaṇḍarohā, holding a vase filled with yellow nectar water. She confers empowerment, and with streams of yellow nectar water she removes what is by nature stain, and poverty, and she worships the person with yellow flowers and the like. Then, inserting so-and-so's life and so on into puṣṭiṃ kuru svāhā, or else into pauṣaṭ, one imagines the person filled with jewels and the like that emerge from the light rays of the mantra being recited, and in three sessions one should recite the mantra with a mind set on increase.

Bringing under control is taught with "red" and so on. The procedure for it is this. Facing west, having given torma, endowed with the yoga of red, two-armed Cakrasaṃvara, and with a mind of passion, one draws the two wheels as before, using red sandalwood, lac and blood from the ring finger, on cloth or else on a leaf of birch bark, upon a woman's lotus, and sets out the mantra in just the same way. In the hub goes phaṭ hoḥ so-and-so's, or so-and-so ba śrīṃ kuru hoḥ. For bringing a woman under control, one inserts the names into bauṣaṭ so-and-so's so-and-so ba śīṃ kuru bauṣaṭ. The wheels are placed inside two unfired bowls, set face to face, wound round with red thread, placed within the wisdom wheel as before, and set in butter or the like as before. Having worshipped them with red flowers and the like, one imagines, in front between the wheel and oneself, the person for whom the rite is done, seated on a red moon maṇḍala. From the light rays of the seed syllable in one's heart emerges red Khaṇḍarohā, holding a noose and a hook. She binds the person's neck with the noose, pierces the heart with the hook and draws the person in, and one imagines that, intoxicated, the person falls at one's feet. One should recite the mantra, with the name inserted, so-and-so basāmānaya hoḥ, in three sessions, for up to three weeks.

Summoning by power is taught with "charnel ground" and so on; it is easy to understand. The procedure of drawing and the rest is just as before. The difference is that the wheel is drawn on the back of a śarabha or a lion. "Insert the syllables hrīḥ jaḥyi" means: for summoning a woman, hrīḥ so-and-so ākarṣaya hrīḥ; for summoning a man, jaḥ so-and-so ākarṣaya jaḥ. The person for whom the rite is done is on a sun maṇḍala. Some say that at the time of recitation one should recite the mantra ending in phaṭ, with the name inserted: so-and-so ākarṣaya jaḥ. At the time of heating, one should recite the mantra and heat the wheel.

The wheel of immobilizing is taught with "Then" and so on. "Joining eight and eight lines evenly" means that, with eight lines drawn from each of the two directions, each side has seven squares, so that the wheel becomes a square made up of forty-nine squares. In it, no mantra letters are to be placed in the squares of the middle row running in each of the two directions: those thirteen squares are empty. The four corners then form nine squares each. In each set of nine squares, in the eight squares surrounding the central square, beginning from the corner square, one writes: oṃ sumbha ni sumbha huṃ hūṃ, oṃ gṛhṇa gṛhṇa huṃ hūṃ, oṃ gṛhṇāpaya gṛhṇāpaya huṃ hūṃ, oṃ ānaya hoḥ bhagavān bidyārāja huṃ hūṃ. In the central squares of the four sets of nine, one should write phaṭ laṃ Devadatta staṃbhaya laṃ as the second line. The two phaṭ syllables come from the words of the glorious Vajraḍāka. At the time of recitation, there is one phaṭ for each line. These are taught with "in the center" and so on.

The procedure for drawing is this. Facing south, having given torma, endowed with the yoga of yellow, two-armed Cakrasaṃvara, one draws, on cloth from a charnel ground, with turmeric juice and orpiment, upon an eight-tiered Mount Meru, two wheels following the arrangement just explained, one running counterclockwise and the other the opposite way. Outside the wheel, in the directions beginning with the east, running counterclockwise, one writes the four lines of the mantra beginning with sumbha, with the name inserted as taught, their heads pointing inward. Outside that one draws three lines. Then the two are placed face to face and enclosed between two bowls, and on top of the upper bowl there is again a Mount Meru. On it one should draw, with laṃ, an earth maṇḍala marked with a crossed vajra arisen from the syllable hūṃ. Then, having wound it round with yellow thread, and after first drawing in the wisdom being, one worships the wheel with yellow flowers and the like. Between the wheel and oneself, one visualizes the person for whom the rite is done as marked by Mount Meru below, above, and in the four directions as well, and one should recite the mantra as taught in three sessions.

Immobilizing speech is taught with "in just that way" and so on. "In just that way" means the six-spoked wheel, as before.

The drawing of the wheel for this is as follows. On cloth from a charnel ground, following the rite of immobilizing, one draws two six-spoked wheels at the heart of a ram, upon a Mahendra maṇḍala, and sets out the seven-syllable mantra as before. In the hub goes phaṭ baṃ Devadatta mukha bandhaya baṃ. Outside the six-spoked wheel, within a square line, one draws, running counterclockwise in the directions beginning with the east, the four lines of the mantra beginning with sumbha, their heads pointing inward, with the name inserted as taught. One sets them in place as before and worships them properly. Then, seeing the person for whom the rite is done seated on a ram between the wheel and oneself, one meditates on yellow Khaṇḍarohā upon that person's tongue, standing on a crossed vajra arisen from the syllable hūṃ, which rests on a Mahendra maṇḍala arisen from the syllable laṃ. One should then recite the mantra beginning with sumbha, with the name inserted, in three sessions.

Killing is taught with "Then" and so on. "Kāka" means a crow. The condensed meaning here is this. At midday or at midnight, in a place strewn with chaff, charcoal and the like, facing toward Yama, that is, the south, one gives torma with a fierce mind. Endowed with the yoga of blue, two-armed Cakrasaṃvara standing on a sun, one draws on cloth from a charnel ground, using poison, salt, pepper, melted butter, mustard seed and the like, with a pen made of a crow's feather or else a pen made of human bone, in the belly of Yama, a wheel of forty-nine squares as before, and sets out on it the mantra beginning with sumbha exactly as before. In the thirteen empty squares one writes: in the first, a long hūṃ; then in the second, Devadatta māraya; then in the third, hūṃ; and in this way one writes in all the squares, running clockwise. By this same procedure one draws the second wheel running counterclockwise. The two are placed face to face and set between two bowls, or else between two skull cups, wound round with blue thread, and the wisdom wheel is drawn into them; then one worships them with blue flowers and the like. Having made present to the mind the meaning of the text from "one should imagine between the wheel and oneself" up to "drinks," one should recite the mantra in three sessions.

These activities too are to be performed only by a yogin who, having carried out the transference from the body, is able to bring about birth in the higher realms and in the pure buddha fields, and is able to summon the consciousness and place it there. And even so, only if one who is harming the Three Jewels does not desist though he is firmly turned back: then, having informed the buddhas and bodhisattvas and having generated great compassion, one should carry it out as taught. For that very reason it says "How wonderful!" and so on. If one asks, "What does someone endowed with such capacity do with a yantra?" that is true; it is in order to care for those who delight in fierce activities. That is stated in the King of Tantras in Two Examinations:

By killing there is no fruit here;
accomplishment is utterly ruined.
Having given up deceiving beings,
do all that is not to be done.
By merely doing harm to beings
one does not gain accomplishment through the mudrā.
All the rites, for the sake of bringing in the wicked,
are fully accomplished from the principal deity.

Dividing is taught with "in that very manner" and so on. "In that very manner" means the procedure of drawing the wheel and the mantra and the rest, just as for killing. The difference is that one wheel should be drawn on the back of a buffalo and the second on the back of a horse. The insertion is phaṭ Devadatta and Yajñadatta bidveśaya phaṭ. Then, as before, one hides it in a charnel ground or at a crossroads. Between the wheel and oneself one visualizes the two persons for whom the rite is done, riding a buffalo and a horse, each holding weapons against the other, and the hosts of wrathful ones emerging from the seed syllable in one's heart driving them into battle; then one should recite the mantra in three sessions.

Expelling is taught with "by that very rite" and so on. "By that very rite" means that, by the rite just explained, one draws two wheels on the back of a camel, inserts hūṃ phaṭ Devadatta uccaṭaya hūṃ phaṭ, and does everything as before. What is then to be done is taught with "one should meditate" and so on, which is easy to understand.

Driving mad is taught with "wrathful" and so on. "Own blood" means the blood of a fox. The meaning of this is as follows. If one wishes to drive the person for whom the rite is done mad, one draws, with poison and the like, on cloth from a charnel ground, at the heart of a monkey, the wheel and the rest just explained, inserts hūṃ phaṭ so-and-so unmanda kuru hūṃ phaṭ, and does everything as before. Then, visualizing that person riding a monkey and dancing, one should recite the mantra in three sessions. The repetition of the passage from "wrathful, facing Yama" up to "in the same way" shows that this rite applies to all fierce activities such as dividing. "Two wheels" and so on teach the rite of drawing the wheels, and these have already been shown. That all of these should be learned only from an authentic guru is taught with "guru" and so on.

This concludes the explanation of the tenth chapter.

To answer the twentieth question, "How should mantra recitation be viewed?" it says "Then" and so on. "Mantra" means the mantras such as the heart mantra of the Blessed One. "Maṇḍala" means the feast gathering. "By the rite taught before" means by the rite taught in the chapter on samaya. "A hundred thousand" is said with reference to the Perfect Age (Kṛtayuga); for the other ages it will be explained. "Mundane and supramundane accomplishment" applies in common to all mantras. By the principal mantra itself, the bringing down of rain and the like is taught. Setting forth the near-heart mantra in particular, it says "to be accomplished" and so on. What is to be done in the approach beforehand and, at the end, at the time of the activity, is taught with "by which" and so on. "By which activity" means pacifying and the rest. "Distinction" means the rite. "Its color and so on" means that one should meditate on the deity and so on with a form, color and the rest in keeping with that activity. That is taught with "seat and" and so on: "seat" means the moon maṇḍala and the rest.

"Countless" means that by reciting the mantra ten million times and so on, the accomplishment of the Great Seal and the accomplishments in keeping with it arise. Therefore the former is only the common accomplishment. "They" means those mantras. "Through certain yoga" means that if one accomplishes them by the rite as taught, one will certainly obtain accomplishment.

This concludes the explanation of the eleventh chapter.

To answer the two questions beginning with the twenty-first, "Of what kind is the rosary? How much is the characteristic of recitation?" it says "Listen" and so on, which is easy to understand. There, "gold and" and so on is the summary. "Should be applied to pacifying and increasing" means that a rosary made of gold, copper and the like is for increasing, and one made of silver, crystal, conch and the like is for the pacifying activity: that is the meaning of "should be applied." "Saffron and the rest, scents and the rest" means that one should anoint it with a form, color, scent and the rest in keeping with the activity; this connects with the preceding "should be applied." Some say it means beads made of saffron and the rest. "Always fifty beads" is because of the purity of āli and kāli, the vowels and consonants. For that very reason, a rosary made of fifty beads is for all activities; for fierce activity, it is made of sixty beads. "By the thumb, the nature of the deity" means that one draws in the beads, which have that nature, with the thumb as with a hook: "it should be applied to all activities. All the beads are worthy of worship" means all the beads whose nature is the recitation mantra and the deity. "The nature of the dharmadhātu" means that the top bead is the form of a dharmadhātu stūpa. In one source it is said that the other beads too should be made to have the nature of the powers, the fearlessnesses and the rest. By these, the consecration of the rosary is indicated. "In equipoise" and so on: the intent is that, once the mind has become stable, one should in the same way, by recitation and meditation, draw in with the thumb the beads of the rosary, whose nature is the deity, and recite as though calling to them with the mantra.

"In the Perfect Age, one is to be recited" means that whatever mantra is said to be recited one hundred thousand times, and also two hundred thousand and so on, one should understand that for the other ages it is double and so on. "The mode of mantra is in four" means the four kinds of mantra recitation. These are shown with "verbal recitation" and so on. "Going and coming" means the going, coming and abiding of the wind. This should be understood in the section on the reality of mantra, in the chapter that teaches the moon, the sun and the five paths; here it is the recitation of those that are relevant. "Liberated from the three extremes" means having abandoned arising, abiding and ceasing. Or else it means that, having abandoned entering, abiding and rising, the wind becomes motionless in the avadhūtī. On this they say the following. When one utters any recitation mantra, such as the heart mantra, from the nāda abiding in the heart of the emanated lotus, it issues from the right, and spreading out in the form of the circle of the maṇḍala, it works for the benefit of beings; from the left it melts and enters. One should meditate that it arises in just that way, and when this has become stable, one obtains the accomplishment of the mantra. Gathering these together, it says "these" and so on, meaning that by these alone the characteristic of mantra recitation and its rite are definitively stated.

This concludes the explanation of the twelfth chapter.

To answer the twenty-third question, "How many circlings of the maṇḍala by the yogin in the form of the deity?" it says "Then" and so on. Here is the stage of meditation. First of all, one generates the awakening mind, thinking, "In order to bring all sentient beings to the state from which glorious Cakrasaṃvara arises, I shall become glorious Cakrasaṃvara." In an instant one makes emptiness manifest. Endowed with the yoga of two-armed Cakrasaṃvara together with his wisdom consort, one resolves that the aggregates, elements and sense sources are, as appropriate, the purities of Vairocana and the rest. Simultaneously with snapping the fingers of the left forefinger, one imagines in the directions beginning with the east, running counterclockwise, the four, blue, green, red and yellow, that arise from the four mantras beginning with sumbha; and at that very same moment, below, the ground whose nature is a crossed vajra, born from the syllable hūṃ, and above, from its radiating light rays, the net of vajra arrows, the vajra tent and the canopy.

In one's own heart, upon a variegated lotus born from a yellow paṃ, on a moon born from the syllable oṃ, by means of the light rays born from a blue hūṃ, one invites the body of the Blessed One, which will be described, together with his retinue, into the space before one. After first prostrating, one worships him properly with the offering goddesses, Puṣpā and the rest, who emerge from the seed syllable in one's heart. "Asking forbearance" means that one should perform the confession of misdeeds and the rest. Then one should meditate on going for refuge to the Three Jewels and the rest, culminating in equanimity: this is the accumulation of merit. "Other beings, equanimity" means that toward other beings, those who have become objects of attachment and of anger, one has equanimity: evenness of mind toward all is what is meant by equanimity.

Then, for the sake of the accumulation of wisdom, one first takes up the meaning of the mantra beginning oṃ svabhāva. There, oṃ is assent. svabhāva śuddhaḥ sarba dharmāḥ refers to the apprehended objects, which are pure by being illusion-like from their very nature. svabhāva śuddho 'haṃ means: just as all phenomena are, so am I. By these the purity of apprehended and apprehender is taught, and this is also emptiness together with appearance. Therefore "one should abide in mind only" means that, since ultimately the entities of apprehended and apprehender are eliminated, these are mind only. And that, too, is oṃ āḥ hūṃ: it abides, ever higher, as the nature of the three syllables. One should realize oneself to have such a nature, and think: "Alas! Under the power of beginningless ignorance, these sentient beings do not see themselves as having such a nature, and wander in saṃsāra like the blind."

"Therefore I shall bring each of them to realize their own nature": generating compassion focused on phenomena in this way, one emanates the three wheels for their sake, makes beings into the three wheels, causes them to melt through great bliss, and absorbs them into that very state. Then the syllable oṃ dissolves into the syllable āḥ, and the syllable āḥ into the syllable hūṃ. That, too, should be absorbed into the nāda, in the sequence of the ū, the ha, the head, the half-moon and the bindu. Then, abiding in the very form of the nāda, and resolving, "Just like this, having made the reality of all phenomena directly manifest, I shall bring all sentient beings to realize it too," one makes the objectless manifest, and casts away even the conception of mere nāda. This is what is taught with "by meditations on the accumulations of awakening," meaning that one should meditate on the accumulation of wisdom. To make it stable, it says "oṃ" and so on.

The stage of arising, by the power of the former aspiration of one who has entered emptiness, is taught with "support and supported" and so on, meaning "upon the support, the bhairaṃpa and the rest." "One should imagine the bhairaṃpa and the rest" means the wind called bhairaṃpa, together with whatever it heads. These are taught in the passage beginning "yaṃ" and ending "adorned," which is easy to understand. "Above it one should meditate on a lotus with hub and filaments": "above it" should be understood from the earlier words. "In its center one should meditate on the yoga" means the moon maṇḍala. "From the purity of āli and kāli" means āli and kāli transformed; here the generation of the moon maṇḍala is set down as "yoga." "Its" and so on is easy to understand. "A head adorned with a garland of skulls" is because each face has five skulls, whose nature is the five tathāgatas. "Should be imagined" means should be meditated on. "In the directional places of the lotus" means on the petals of the directions. Although these deities, ending with Yamamathanī, are complete at exactly the same moment that the Blessed One is complete, they are taught in sequence because a single conceptual meditation cannot take them all as its object at once, just as the arms, faces and the rest are taught in sequence.

Then one meditates on the wisdom being in the heart of the Blessed One, complete in every aspect, and on the samādhi being in its heart, and blesses them with oṃ āḥ hūṃ oṃ sarba bīra yoginīnāṃ kāya bāk citta bajra svabhāva ātmako 'haṃ and oṃ bajra śuddhāḥ sarba dharmā bajra śuddho 'haṃ. On the six, the eyes and the rest, one meditates on the six, Mohavajra and the rest; on earth and the rest, Pātanī and the rest; and on the aggregate of form and the rest, Vairocana and the rest. One puts two armors on the Blessed One and the Blessed Lady: the first is oṃ ha and so on, the second oṃ baṃ and so on. Some say that at the places beginning with the crown, on sun maṇḍalas in twenty-four figures of thousand-spoked wheels, there are also the syllables pū ja and the rest.

After that, preceded by the formation of lotus and vajra, one begins the supreme joy. By the light rays of the seed syllable in one's heart, and also by the blazing mudrā and the syllable phaiṃ, one invites the wisdom wheel. Having performed the summoning and the rest with the mantras jaḥ hūṃ baṃ hoḥ, one pleases it with the embracing mudrā, preceded by the lotus-turning gesture. One blesses with the three mantras as before, and has oneself empowered by the heroes and the heroine sovereign ladies who have come together with the wisdom wheel. Immediately upon the empowerment, on the crowns of the Blessed One and the Blessed Lady are Akṣobhya and Vairocana. On the crowns of Ḍākinī, Lāmā, Khaṇḍarohā and Rūpiṇī are, respectively, Vairocana, Ratnasambhava, Amitābha and Amoghasiddhi. For Kākāsyā and the rest it is Amoghasiddhi alone. Preceded by this sealing, one worships and praises them with the offering goddesses who emerge from the seed syllable in one's heart, tastes the flavor drawn forth by the goddess Rasanā, and gradually enters luminosity.

The sequence for this is as follows. Beings, perfectly summoned by the sound of the joy of the wisdom being, father and mother, dissolve into the charnel grounds; the charnel grounds into the samaya wheel; that into Ḍākinī and the rest; and those into the Blessed One and the Blessed Lady alone. These too gradually enter luminosity, and then come abiding in luminosity, rising and entering. If one becomes weary, one recites the mantra by the rite taught in the chapter on mantra. If one asks, "When one wishes to rise, what should one do?" it says "Dharma and" and so on. One meditates on Ḍākinī and the rest in the wheels of dharma and the rest. In the mouth, the left nostril, the anus and the right nostril one places Kākāsyā and the rest; in the right ear and the other ear, the right eye and the other eye, one places Yamadāḍhī and the rest. At the crown and the other places, as before, one should also ascertain the twenty-four channels and the elements in union with them, the teeth, nails and the rest. The secret places of the Blessed One and the Blessed Lady, and their union, should be learned from the guru's instructions. Then, viewing oneself and all the variety as having the nature of great bliss, one should enjoy bliss as one wishes.

Gathering all this together, it says "thus" and so on: the nature of the body as taught is the hero, and one becomes equal to all the buddhas. If one asks why this is so, it says "nondual" and so on, meaning that through the yoga of that nature of great bliss, nondual with emptiness, the inconceivable state of buddhahood is shown. For that very reason: if by meditating on the generation stage, the elaborated stage, as one's path, one does not even attain samādhi, how then could one attain buddhahood? For that would contradict both reasoning and scripture.

Moreover, it is like this. For the time being, one is to meditate in stages, making the mind stable in the manner of imagining a wheel of many deities whose nature is the support and the supported. Even when one meditates with intense conviction that they are all complete together in a single instant, at the time one meditates on the face, there is no meditation on the arms and so forth; and likewise, at the time one meditates on the other limbs, the arms and so forth, one after another, there is no meditation on the face and so forth. And if they are not meditated upon, the face and so forth are not clear, for it is established by the self-awareness of all people that two conceptions do not occur at once. If one asks why this is so, it is because samādhi, whose defining characteristic is one-pointedness of mind, is disturbed by many conceptions.

Furthermore, buddhahood is the fruit of the accumulations of merit and wisdom; how could it come about through mere meditation? Otherwise some pauper, by meditating "I am a universal monarch," would attain the state of a universal monarch, and by meditating on a hungry ghost one would become a hungry ghost; for it would follow that virtuous and non-virtuous actions are without results.

Granted, by meditation an object may become somewhat clear; even so, like the image of a desired woman becoming clear to a lustful man, it stays connected only for a moment. Because it is imagined, it is false in nature: worldly things, arising as they do from clinging to what is not real, are in this way impermanent. Imagined yoga is even more false than worldly things, because by it one likewise meditates even on sounds and the like, which are devoid of color and shape.

And even if what has become clear were to remain for an instant, it would serve no purpose. For things with form abide in a specific place and time. And because it makes no special difference, it lacks the qualities, such as the superknowledges, that are needed for the welfare of beings. The pervasive are those that possess qualities of that kind. What has form is not pervasive, for otherwise it would follow that a pot, too, is pervasive.

And if it is held that whatever is meditated upon becomes clear, then one would also have to accept that, like a jewel of many forms, the many forms desired by many disciples of differing inclinations become vividly clear all at once. For that very reason, since the enjoyment bodies and emanation bodies, endowed with immeasurable various forms according to the different inclinations of infinite disciples, cannot be meditated upon, one should strive only at their cause. And that is not mere meditation.

Thus, first of all, by reasoning, buddhahood does not come from meditating on the elaborated aspect. Nor does it by scripture. For as it says in the glorious Vajraḍāka:

"Thus, in the sādhanas composed by following the tantras,
people with great effort stir up afflictions
and destroy their own minds.
Therefore, for all one's efforts,
the actions of rites such as the maṇḍala
will not bring liberation;
they are causes only of delusion."

And in the glorious King of Tantras in Two Examinations, too:

"There is no mantra recitation, no austerity, no fire offering;
no master of the maṇḍala, nor any maṇḍala either.
That is mantra recitation, that is austerity, that is fire offering;
that is the master of the maṇḍala, and that is the maṇḍala too.
In brief, the assembly is that which has the form of mind."

And from the glorious Sampuṭatilaka, too:

"One should not meditate on it as empty,
nor meditate on it as nonempty either;
for from grasping at the nonempty or the empty,
conceptual thought of another kind will arise."

Indrabhūtipāda, too, said:

"Mudrā, maṇḍala, mantra and the rest,
the striving of recitation and meditation:
even in countless tens of millions of eons
they will not bring the supreme accomplishment."

Sarahapāda, too, said:

"No mantra, no cause, no grasping at knowable things;
oh, all of these are causes of delusion and confusion!
Do not stain the stainless mind with meditation;
do not torment the mind that abides in bliss,"

and so forth.
