"All" means every one, and "become rivers" means the five kinds of rivers. "Going in the middle" means going through the middle of them, that is, the Gaṅgā. "Is called the ocean of milk": these teach the outer. "Of the drink of the moon" means of the nature of the drink of the moon. "Body": in the body dwells the Blessed Lady Vajravārāhī. If one asks how she dwells, it says, "Of Vairocanī," and so on; the meaning is that the Blessed One Heruka and Vairocanī, dwelling in mutual embrace, melted through great passion.

Those very things it states with "All heroes," and so on. "Of all become one" means things whose nature is the stable and the moving; it is all having become one, or else it is all the channels themselves. The nectar is liberation, whose nature is innate joy. Because this very Blessed Lady makes one fearless toward the māras of the afflictions and the rest, she is the fierce Rūpiṇī. From the crown wheel of these, the awakening mind, melted by its very nature of great bliss, pervades all the channels by way of the avadhūtī, and because it and the Blessed Lady together generate great joy, she is Rūpiṇī in the form of liquor. As it is said: "That which falls day and night as streams of white drops is not liquor; it is Māmakī." And in this very tantra too:

The Blessed One has the form of semen;
his bliss is remembered as the Lady of Desire.

Because of that very non-difference shown by these words, in some places bliss and the awakening mind are taught as having the nature of the Blessed One. In some places this is also taught in the masculine gender and in the feminine gender, and, with regard to bliss alone, in the neuter as well.

"Robbing" means suffering and the like. "Doing" means joy and the like. "Enjoyment": from the very nature of mind, mind alone has the nature of great bliss, and the nectar of its essence is likewise. Showing that, conventionally, just as there is nectar within the body, so too there is outside, it says, "The hearth," and so on. Teaching the purpose, it says, "Without liquor," and so on: wisdom is what is beyond the world, and consciousness is what is worldly.

Therefore "wisdom and consciousness perfected," and "beings everywhere deluded": by these words its nature is proclaimed. The special purpose too is taught with "Sites," and so on; the meaning is that, when one has gone to the sites and the rest, this is supreme for the sake of worship. If one asks, "Is this, then, the correct view of all the treatises?" it says, "Tantra and," and so on. Setting out the procedure of conduct, it says, "Especially," and so on: "doing especially" is for the sake of the drink of the yogin, and of the gurus, the heroes, and the yoginīs.

It is taught as follows. On the right hand, visualize a wheel arisen from the syllable hoḥ, of the nature of Vairocana and consecrated by the syllable hoḥ. Above it, on the vessel of the substance, cover it with the left hand, visualized as an eight-petaled red lotus arisen from the syllable ha, consecrated by the syllable ha, and of the nature of Amitābha. Looking upon the syllable hrīḥ, of the nature of Akṣobhya, resting on a sun above the substance, recite the mantra ha hoḥ hrīḥ three, five, or seven times. Recite in just the same way the mantra oṃ āḥ hūṃ, its syllables placed in turn on the right, on the left, and in front above the substance. Having pleased the gurus, the heroes, and the yoginīs by the rite of pleasing, one should then partake oneself.

The fault of partaking without having done so is taught with "The three deities," and so on: the three deities are oṃ and the rest, whose nature is Vairocana, Amitābha, and Akṣobhya. "Hostile to the guru, reviling the guru, hostile to sentient beings": one should not give it to these; the meaning is that these should not be admitted to the pledge. That one should not deceive those who hold the pledge is taught with "Yoga," and so on. The result of partaking according to the rite is taught with "Wisdom," and so on.

In order to teach the principal preparation by classifying it, it says, "Substances," and so on. The substances are myrobalan and the like. The roots are those of the various medicines. "Tree-born" means mango and the like. The procedure for the principal preparation is taught with "Ananta," and so on. Śigru is śaubhañjana. "Pāsthāma" means sixteen palas. "Bhūta flower" is what is well known as "bhūta hair." Malaya is sandalwood. "Śāriva" is utpalaśāriva, known in the world as acchu atti. "Krānta" is viṣṇukrānta; some say it is sudarśana. "With a quarter part" means water; the meaning is eight palas each of dhātakī and the rest. Seb-ya is uśīra. "Māgadha" is long pepper. "Water" means sixteen palas. Cakra is sudarśana. "Possessed of bees" means honey; the meaning is that juice of śigru root, strained through cloth, eight times the amount of the honey, should be boiled down until only the measure of the honey remains. "By a hundredth part" means honey. "Piercing" means inserting. "Bee" means from bees, and "produced from it" means honey. That too is fourfold: saubhañjana and goat water should be boiled down until only the measure of the honey remains. "Of two kinds" means twofold. "Tuhina" means tuhina camphor and the like. "When accomplished, with a quarter remaining" means with a fourth left over. "Fragrant substances" means those just mentioned. "As the sequence is accomplished by that very thing" means it is accomplished, prepared month by month. "Following the region" should be understood as: liquor follows the region. Region stands for more than itself: it also covers the season. The intent is that in a region and a season of little warmth one should not make liquor finished in a single day.

"Offerings" means the offerings, such as a fire offering without ghee. "Improper, not possible otherwise" means this is improper: the meaning is that, other than as taught, no particular reasoning about its offerings and the rest is possible. Some volumes read "liberation": liberation itself is not possible otherwise, meaning that it would be devoid of the Dharma. "From rice" means liquor produced from rice; the words to be supplied are "is not supreme." "Wherever" means anywhere at all: it does not become a pledge substance. An intoxicant whose principal ingredient is rice or the like is not superior in taste, potency, or digestion, and so it is not fit to be a pledge substance. The intent, therefore, is that the kinds specified should be prepared. Some volumes read "possible otherwise." In that case the meaning is that apart from the intoxicant no pledge is possible anywhere. Therefore, showing that guarding the pledges and pleasing the guru is the intoxicant, it says, "Oneself," and so on. "Some" means that a yogin attains worldly and transworldly aims through the merit of guarding the pledges and through pleasing the guru, and that too not without the pledge substance; by this it is specially taught that one must without fail make the pledge substance primary.

Here, then, is the procedure for brewing. Set out the medicines as taught, according to the place, the season, and their potency. The mantra practitioner, together with his wisdom consort, having first given a torma and being endowed with the yoga of his own special deity, should in front of himself place a pot on a seat of bundled grass and the like, which has the nature of the three nāga lords Ananta, Vāsuki, and Varuṇa. Fumigate the pot with guggulu incense and worship it with flowers. Put into it the kneaded lump of śaubhañjana and the rest, mixed with water. Then visualize within the pot, in an instant, glorious Cakrasaṃvara embraced by glorious Vajravārāhī, together with the retinue, melting into great bliss; from this arises the syllable maṃ, and from that the goddess of liquor, whose nature is as taught. One should resolve with special conviction that the nectar of wisdom, whose nature is great bliss, abides with the nature of semen, or as form itself, and should continually perform the emanation and the rest. That is what is meant. This is the explanation of the twenty-sixth chapter.

In order to answer the question about the extraction of mantras, it says, "From this," and so on. "Desire" means the thirteenth. "Fierce" means the eleventh. "Openings" means nine. "Sages" means seven. "Arrows" means the fifth.

"Form" means one; this should be understood as lying beyond fire. "Fire" means three. "From the instruction" refers to the oral instruction "beginning from the first cell of the row made up of thirteen cells." "Of the first cell" means the set of the first row of cells. "Fifth" means what is located in the fifth cell. There, "the first cell itself should be given above" means that whatever is located in the first cell of the first row of cells should be given above. It is the same when some read "the single cell." Then, when the vowel is strengthened, it becomes the syllable oṃ. What is stated there as "by the eleventh cell": "from that" means "in that," that is, the syllable oṃ located in the eleventh place, counted in the order of subtracting from the cells. By placing it above, the vowel is strengthened together with it, and it becomes the syllable oṃ together with the half-moon, by the joining of sounds itself. Therefore the third in the row of cells numbered nine, the letter ma, in the form of a drop, should be given above the half-moon. The rest is easy to understand.

Here, first is the ten-syllable heart mantra of the Blessed One. Then comes the seven-syllable near-heart mantra. Then comes the thirteen-syllable heart mantra of the goddess. Then comes the four-line mantra. The extraction of these should be learned from the guru. The hidden intent of this is stated with "The volume," and so on. If one asks when these extractions of mantras should be made, it says, "The tenth," and so on.

To make a distinction between the mantras and the deities is a breach of the pledge, and that too should not be done; so it says, "Life," and so on. Giving up the disparagement of wisdom is taught with "To the worldly," and so on. "Satisfying and eating with the left" means that one satisfies and eats with the left hand as well. Forbidding the abandonment of great love is taught with "The five families," and so on. "All conceptions definitely released" means free of conceiving a beginning and so on with regard to all phenomena, which are empty of beginning, middle, and end. "All duality abandoned" means that one should not make a distinction between the five aggregates and the five Tathāgatas. Giving up disrespect and disparagement toward the guru is taught with "The guru," and so on; "all" and so on are qualities of the guru.

"By these the root downfalls are entirely abandoned": one should know the fourteen root downfalls, as many as are shown in the chapter on empowerment, and also all those taught in other tantras. They are as follows:

Showing contempt for the holy guru,
transgressing the word of the perfect Buddha,
speaking in anger of the faults of a vajra sibling,
giving up great love,
entirely giving up the awakening mind,
disparaging the three vehicles or others,
proclaiming the secret to the unripened,
disparaging the aggregates, which are the buddhas themselves,
doubting what is pure by nature,
showing love to the malicious at the beginning, the end, and between,
conceiving this or that in emptiness,
discouraging the mind of one who has faith,
not relying on the pledges as they are obtained,
and disparaging women: these are the fourteen.
For a downfall in any one of these,
one is purified by receiving empowerment again.

These are the root downfalls.

Taking her by the force of wisdom,
and likewise taking the awakening mind,
not hiding the secret from the unfortunate,
quarreling in the maṇḍala of the feast,
speaking of the Dharma otherwise to those with faith,
staying seven days among the śrāvakas,
the falsehood of a yogin's pride,
and teaching the Dharma to those without faith:
these are the eight gross downfalls.
If one has broken them, one should confess;
this is to be known from the rite.
This is the sequence of the root and the gross downfalls.

That is what is meant. This is the explanation of the twenty-seventh chapter.

In order to answer the question about forceful subjugation and taking into care, it says, "Then," and so on. "Mantra" means the heart mantra of the Blessed One. "In the fire of an outcaste" means in a fire taken from the house of an outcaste. "Half the night" means at midnight. "The fire of the piśācas" means the fire of a charnel ground. "Situated in the direction above the wind" means riding on a wind maṇḍala.

It is taught as follows. One who wishes to summon should face the direction of the wind and give a torma. Endowed with the yoga of red Cakrasaṃvara and standing in the playful posture, visualize Khaṇḍarohā, who emerges from the seed syllable at one's heart, binding by the neck the person to be won over, who rides upon a wind maṇḍala, with the noose of her hands that hold a hook and a noose, piercing the heart with the hook and drawing that person in. Then recite whatever mantra is desired, such as the heart mantra interspersed with "jaḥ so-and-so ākarṣaya jaḥ." On birch bark, with blood from one's own ring finger and with gorocanā, using a pen made of thangphrom wood, draw a likeness of the person to be won over, and place it in a beautiful skull cup that is undamaged and in one piece. Cover it with cloth, and above it make a fire offering of red karavīra flowers, each preceded by recitation of the mantra; then the summoning will take place.

"Then, again," means another application of summoning as well; it is easy to understand. "Having drawn a bhaga": the word to be supplied is "in front." "With the left hand" should be understood as "having pressed down." "An upward-rising whirlwind" means on a wind maṇḍala. "The cloth of a preta" means a shroud. "The nāga lamp" means a lamp bowl made of nāgakesara wood; some say it is a lamp bowl of lead. "Elephant dung" means together with an elephant. This is the explanation of the twenty-eighth chapter.

In order to answer the four questions beginning "The realities," it says, "Then," and so on. There, the passage from "Meditation" up to "Victor" teaches reality. The passage from "By the two" up to "the shape of Heruka" teaches the division of reality into emptiness and compassion. The two verses beginning "Without appearance" fully teach how all the things apprehended by these are without intrinsic nature, being the opposing side itself. The passage from "Likewise" up to "four" again fully teaches that, because these are free of elaboration, the nature of the defining mark of the body of great bliss is free of conceptual thought.

Its essence, it is said, is distinguished by the division made through exclusion as innate joy itself, whose other name is the stage of luminosity, the nature of the meaning of the fourth empowerment. "As it is" means unerring; that itself is an unfabricated thing, that is, it is without intrinsic nature. "Meditating on the very nature of existence" means having the nature of meditating on existence itself. Moreover, "its essential nature is no thing" means that it is without intrinsic nature. If one asks what is without intrinsic nature, it is what is free of elaboration; the meaning is that it is free of every movement of conceptual thought.

Someone may object: "Granted that the imagined external objects that are grasped become non-things, how do form and mind, elaborated by the very nature of self-awareness, become so?" In reply it says, "Self-aware," and so on: consciousness too is just the same. A thing is what performs a function, and that is differentiated by division into past, future, and present. Of these, what is past has perished without continuing. The future is like the son of a barren woman. The present too is simply without intrinsic nature. If one asks why, it says, "One and," and so on: the defining mark of present consciousness, too, is simply devoid of the nature of one or not one. For it would have to be either with parts or partless. If it has parts, it is not free of the fault of being composed of atoms. If it is partless, it is simply like space. "From the instant itself" means from the previous one. "Arisen" means conventionally. That very point it states with "Self-aware," and so on: it is not something merely imputed, like the horn of a rabbit. It is beyond the range of speech. Showing a few synonyms of this, such as reality, the all-empty, the stage of luminosity, and the Dharma body, it says, "Omniscient," and so on; this is easy to understand.

If one asks, "Then are these not of the nature of bliss?" it says, "This," and so on. "The arising of the bliss of the buddhas is from this" means the arising of the bliss of the buddhas. "In which" means from which ultimate truth; it is shown as the very ground of all phenomena. "In some" means in some other places. "The abode of awakening" means the site of awakening. Incomparable, equal in each case, just that itself, is the defining mark of suchness, as the Victors have taught. The intent is: "I have not taught it elsewhere, but here I have made it clear."

"Two": the two are grasped and grasper, thing and non-thing, saṃsāra and nirvāṇa, the conventional and the ultimate, and so on. That in which these are abandoned is clear, because it is fully indicated by the nature of great bliss; its clarity comes from the ever-changing waves of the joy of elaboration. "Perfect awareness": by that one is fortunate. "Captivating" means the person who has gathered the special qualities. "Collection" means uninterrupted joy. "Fully interwoven" means that in which it abides interwoven, just so. "Supreme power" means complete and pervasive with the taste of what does not turn back. "The center of the sky" means just as space is not superimposed upon. "By manifold elaborations" means pervading, gathered by emanations of many kinds. "Become abiding in the whole of space" means abiding by giving room to all of space, just so. The meaning is that to a mind that has become thus, it is fully clear, as if it had dissolved into the three worlds.

"Nature" means nature. "Stated" means in the third chapter. "Maṇḍala": this very perfect awareness is called the taking of the essence, which has the perfect nature of all phenomena. "Its characteristic" means the supreme characteristic, superior to all the characteristics of the aggregates and the rest. Or else it means "by this characteristic which is superior in every aspect," that is, "supreme in characteristic in every aspect." It is the body adorned and ornamented with the thirty-two major marks and the eighty excellent minor marks. "Ḍākinīs" means the qualities such as the applications of mindfulness; these are its channels. By these it is said that what is perfected from the atoms of all the qualities of a buddha, such as the factors of awakening, is not the body.

That very point it states with "All," and so on: "all" means of all. "Without appearance" means realizing that appearances such as the stable and the solid are not apprehended, just so. "Without opening" means free of orifices and the like. "Without remainder" means nothing is left over, because even the habitual tendencies are gone without remainder. "Without abode" is because it is free of things that are support and supported.

If one asks why it is first set out in this way, it says, "Nature," and so on. "In which" means "from which." "Formless" means because nothing is apprehended, by the division of there being nothing to grasp. "The joy that is made" means the joy that has arisen. "Likewise that mind is without mind" means that, being free of thinking and of the elaboration of conceptual thought, it is without mind, that is, non-conceptual. As it is said in the Blessed Lady (the Perfection of Wisdom): "That mind exists, the mind that is inconceivable." "One" means non-dual. "Realizes the various" shows all directions and times.

These very things it states with "The thing," and so on. "In which" means: that in which there is the nature free of conceptual thought, with the characteristic stated, is called the fourth; it is the well-known innate joy, and this is to be connected with "clear." "The great self" is that which is endowed with the greatness of realizing and understanding those activities as they are, just so. How else is it? "Pervading the endless extent of space." How else does it become? Because it is isolated from thing and non-thing, it is not endowed with any separate thing. How else does it become? "It emanates a maṇḍala like a mirror, by its stainless continuum": from the very continuum in which the reflections of beings of every kind appear, it emanates a maṇḍala like a mirror. "Pervading in all aspects" is a qualification meaning that to pervade in all aspects is its manner. Or else it reads "free of all aspects," the intent being that it is free of attachment to them everywhere and free of stains. How else does it become? It abides by accomplishing the ultimate excellence of all blisses; that is, it is the ground. Or else "in which" is said with reference to innate joy.

Therefore this is the meaning: that in whose presence the collection of aspects of qualities is thus accomplished should be understood as the innate, which culminates in the fourth and becomes the fruit; the other is a mere indication. That very point is stated in the passage beginning "Perfect"; it is easy to understand. The conduct of meditating on union is stated in the passage beginning "Free of attachment." If one asks to whom this stage should be taught, it is stated in the passage beginning "Virtuous." That one who has become thus through this yoga, by constant familiarization alone, attains the fruit of all outer activities is stated in the passage beginning "Likewise." This is the explanation of the twenty-ninth chapter.

One who lacks the capacity to meditate on reality as it has been taught should meditate on the form of the deity by meditating on the generation stage. And that cannot be done without seeing an image. Implied by this point, only painted scrolls and statues that possess the proper characteristics are the cause of the accumulations of merit and wisdom, while their opposite is the cause of faults, both seen and unseen, as will be explained in the passage beginning "The three long ones." Elsewhere too it is said:

An image endowed with every characteristic,
possessing the moods of peace and the rest,
beautiful with measure and sub-measure:
in such a one consecration takes effect.
Images made in the world
that are deficient in measure and sub-measure
bring sickness and death and destroy wealth,
and likewise cause separation from kin.
Beautiful in all its parts,
the supreme pacifier of all,
a form neither deficient nor excessive,
possessing the stated measures, should be worshipped.
Rightly endowed with the nature of form and shape,
with compassion, seat and graceful poise,
and with the colors of its place:
in that the deity comes to be present.

Therefore the characteristics of painted scrolls and the like are stated in the passage beginning "Then." "Extent" means length and breadth. "The line of Brahmā before the figure": at the outset of making the figure, "the line of Brahmā" is the Brahmā line, the central axis. "Five parts are the seat": it is five spans in breadth, and the figure stands in the posture with the right leg extended. "The body with three segments": a segment is a bend, the meaning being that the body has three bends. That is stated: "The shoulders level with the face," meaning also in the face and the two shoulders. Therefore the seat, the body and the neck are also bent, because the neck and the body are the very supports of the face and shoulders.

The point that, having laid down the Brahmā line, one should divide the face and so on is stated in the passage beginning "Face." Whatever the size of the image, a cubit or the like, one takes its measure. A tenth of it, the extent of the face, a palm and a span are synonyms. And a twelfth of the face, a minor part, a digit and a finger are one in meaning. Throughout, the divisions should be understood in terms of minor parts. For the Blessed One, one should understand what is stated in the Characteristics of Images and elsewhere: "Following the very figure of the Buddha, one should make a face of twelve and a half minor parts." "Twelve parts" is stated as common to all figures other than the Buddha.

"Four digits for the neck": the Blessed One's neck is four digits. "The head and hairline likewise": the place of the head and the height of the hair are likewise, meaning four minor parts. "For the two ears": the two ears too are likewise in length, four and a half minor parts. That is stated: "well marked" means having the supreme characteristics. "The face is of three parts": the face has three parts, each part of four minor parts. Of these, one part is the forehead, the second the nose, and the third the mouth; here "mouth" designates the area from below the nose down to the chin. For the Blessed One this becomes four and a half digits, since his face is of twelve and a half minor parts.

"Of the edge of the brows and the upper face": below the brows and above the mouth there are two digits in breadth, meaning the width of the eyes. "Marked at four digits": the meaning is that four digits are marked straight across for the eyes. "Two palms from the central Vasu portion": the meaning is that from the edge of the neck, which is eight minor parts in breadth, there is one face-length straight to each of the two shoulders, leaving out on each side the portion of two minor parts of swelling flesh. Even where it is read "from the deity portion," the deity is Vasu alone.

"The heart and the place of the navel": from the base of the neck to the heart, and from the heart to the place of the navel, is the measure of a palm. "Between the nipples is a palm": between the two nipples is one palm. "From the nipples, the chest likewise": above the nipples, up to the two armpits, the chest is like the palm between the neck and the navel, meaning twenty-five minor parts. Or else, from the nipples, facing toward the back, straight along the side, is one palm.

"The arms are of three palms": the arms extend from the armpit as far as the wrist, each arm separately being of three palms. "With a palm, its two hands": each of the two hands is the measure of a palm. "Thighs and shanks rightly endowed, with four digits": setting aside the kneecap of three digits, the thighs and shanks, together with the junctions of two minor parts each above and below the knee, are rightly endowed, meaning that they possess the measure of the two arms. Thus from the root of the thigh to the tip of the shank there come to be fifty-seven minor parts. "The portions are four digits": the portions of swelling flesh, two minor parts each, make four minor parts. Or else, below the ankle one should make four minor parts for the heel. "The sole likewise": the foot too has that same palm measure, meaning after setting aside six digits for the heel. "In drawing the figure of a deity of twelve palms with the sole": the meaning is that in drawing the figure of an ordinary deity it comes to twelve palm units. Or else, the remainder is: "One should draw a deity of twelve palms together with the topknot of matted hair and the seat."

What palms and minor parts are to be given to the seat is stated: "The remainder of four digits." The meaning is that, setting aside the remaining four digits, the seat, which is said to be Bhairava and Kālarātri, should be made eight digits high. If one asks what is to be done with the four minor parts set aside, it says: "Of the seat and the connection of the seat, of the two, a digit and digits." The meaning is that the seats, the sun and the multicolored lotus, are joined, being visible at one digit and at three. Or else, since the Blessed One Akṣobhya resides upon it, the seat is the crown of matted locks. "Seat" here, the seat of this one upon a seat, meaning seat upon seat, is the crest jewel, two digits high; the meaning is that one should make the crown of matted locks joined to it. That very point is stated by way of the distinctive feature of the crown of matted locks: "With a moon disk at its heart," meaning a crown of matted locks adorned with the crescent moon.

"Twelve palms is the characteristic": that the characteristic measure in palms is twelve parts sums up the meaning of the preceding points. Or else, the twelve palms from the edge of the seat to the end of the matted locks are the characteristic of the Blessed One, the purity of the twelve profound grounds. "Low": the meaning is that one should draw all the limbs and lesser limbs, low, high and thick, with perfect grace. "And that": that falls within the palm measure. The intent is that one should not abandon the palm measure as it has been stated and devise it according to one's own wish.

"Equal in the two arms": the meaning is that both arms should be made equal in shape. "To be removed" is the brief statement that, when drawing a peaceful deity, two minor parts each are to be removed. "And that" means "of that, too." "The belly should always be made pleasing": the meaning is that the belly, facing forward, brings forth joy and faith. The brief statement is specified in the passage beginning "To be set aside": at each of the junctions two minor parts each are cut off. The intent, therefore, is that for bodhisattvas and the other peaceful deities, by subtracting two and a half minor parts each from the upper and lower body, the body comes to a measure of one hundred and twenty minor parts.

The characteristics of the wrathful ones and others are stated in the passage beginning "Wrathful." "Ten palms" should be understood as reckoned from the edge of the seat to the end of the noose of hair. Deities that are short with pendulous bellies are said to be of eight palms. Graceful wrathful deities are of ten palms. "A beautiful and charming form" is a captivating form. "Nine palms": the body should be made of nine palms. The meaning is that the goddesses of the maṇḍala, such as Vajravārāhī, and noble Tārā and the others, are also made of nine palms. That very point is stated in the passage beginning "Sequence," the intent being that all deities should be drawn by this sequence.

Here the summarized meaning is this. Having laid down a Brahmā line of the full size of the image to be made, establish a tenth of it as the palm. Then divide that again into twelve and a half minor parts and fix one portion of it as a minor part. At its center the height is four digits. Here too minor part, digit and finger are synonyms, and an eighth of it is termed a barley grain. Likewise, below the head the forehead is four minor parts; below that the nose is four minor parts; and below that, down to the end of the chin, one should allot four and a half minor parts.

Then the forehead measures eight digits straight across, four digits on each side of the Brahmā line; some say nine digits. The eyebrows, running horizontally from the lower edge of the forehead, start on both sides of the Brahmā line at the end of one minor part less a quarter on each side; adding a minor part of the forehead, they curve like a bow, as is beautiful, four minor parts in length and two barley grains thick at the middle. Below that, setting aside half a minor part on each side of the Brahmā line, the eyelid, the fleshy skin that covers the eye, which is called the eye cover, rises in sequence with respect to the inner and outer corners to one digit less a quarter at the middle, like a lotus petal. With measures of three minor parts in breadth above and four in length across, and setting aside two digits in the middle for the space below the brows, this is the line of the eye-opening.

The eyes run horizontally for four minor parts from the end of the half minor part on each side of the Brahmā line, together with the half minor part of each of the inner and outer corners. Of these, the one near the nose is the inner corner, and the other is the outer corner. These two are also the roots of the white round; setting them aside, the white round is three digits wide and two digits high. At its center the black round, called the seeing eye, is one minor part all around. At its center is the pupil, one fifth of a minor part. The sharp eye, otherwise named the rim, is also equal to the pupil. The form that supports the eye-opening, called the mudga of the eye, is raised by a quarter of a minor part. The eye bulge in height, beginning from the inner part below the middle of the brow and ending at the base of the eye-opening, that is, what the word "bulge" denotes, is two digits. Here the manner accords with the mood: closed, wide open, and so on.

Or else, the eyes of all buddhas, gazing in meditative concentration, are two barley grains in breadth and two digits and six barley grains in length, in the shape of a bow. Those of bodhisattvas are four barley grains in breadth and twenty in length, like a blue lotus. The eyes of the principal face of the Blessed One glorious Cakrasaṃvara are ten barley grains in breadth and fourteen in length, and round; so too are those of the other wrathful deities. The eyes of the right and left faces, again, are one digit in breadth and two digits in length, like a lotus. The eyes of the western face are six barley grains in breadth and eighteen in length, shaped like a fish's belly; so too are those of women in whom the erotic mood predominates.

Between the eyes the root of the nose is one minor part in both width and height. The tip is two digits wide and one and a half digits high. The nostrils are each half a minor part across and one minor part high. Between the two nostrils the septum, straight across, is half a minor part.

The tip of the nose, by the measure of its rounded curve, is one digit and a barley grain. The two nostrils each spread by two barley grains. Below the nose, the lip, beginning from the edge of the nose, projects, and its depth from the root of the teeth is also one minor part. Horizontally its width is one minor part on each side of the Brahmā line. Above the lip, the center being low, is a minor part less a quarter, like a gocchi khati. Below the gocchi, at the tip of the upper lip, level with the Brahmā line, the width straight across is two minor parts, the center slightly raised. Below that is the lower lip, of the same kind: its projection and depth are one minor part each, and its width straight across is two minor parts, and at the center, level with the Brahmā line, it is slightly low, which is called the line. At the portions at the two ends of the two lips, the corners of the mouth are one minor part each, spreading by one barley grain. At the very end they spread slightly more than a barley grain, so that in sum the mouth is four digits. Here the lines of the eyeballs, the chin and the corners of the mouth should be made level, and likewise the inner corners of the eyes and the nostrils.

Below the lower lip the chin is two digits long. Projecting from the root of the neck and extending to the end of the level line of the ends of both corners of the mouth, it is four digits across. At the tip of the chin the width across is only two digits. From the outer end extending along the line of the chin, as far as the root of the ear, is what the word "jaw," the side of the mouth, denotes: six digits in length and two and a half digits in breadth. Beginning from the edge of the ear, at the outer end of the place of the jaw's width, the round of the cheek is four digits in length and breadth. Another term for the cheek is the rounded part below the eye, extending to the end of the width of the nose; it too is three digits, raised one digit all around.

From the root of the jaw, the ear is four and a half digits in length; it should be made so that the line level with the curve of the eyebrows becomes the top of the ear, and it spreads two digits straight across. There, the part shaped like a berkari, below the coil of the ear, lying five and a half digits from the corner of the eye, emerges from the edge of the jaw like the petal of an opened flower; in width straight across and in projection it is half a minor part, and in length one minor part. Below that, above the root of the lobe, is the kākāni, like a petal of the ear. Beyond that, the ear hole is half a minor part, projecting one minor part. Beyond that, the remaining part of one minor part, like a stone basin, is one minor part in height, projection and width. Above the kākāni is a part two digits in upward measure and one digit in width, shaped like a hole. Beyond the part like a stone basin is what is called the petal of the ear, and on this side of it is the part of the ear called the śakuli, two barley grains straight across and in spread, lying as though encircling the stone basin. Above the part like a stone basin, too, one should make one and a half digits in upward measure and one and a quarter digits in width. The coil of the ear, encircling the ear, made of skin, like a berkari, is a quarter of a minor part in breadth and height. From the remaining part, the back of the ear projects one and a half digits; the ear ornament, the ear tilaka, faces forward as is beautiful, its width one and a half minor parts. The petal of the ear is likewise.

With the ears facing forward, the head between them is eighteen digits; at the back it is fourteen digits. For the Blessed One, even with four faces or three faces, it should be understood that there is no back portion; in sum the circumference of the head is thirty-two minor parts. Therefore the straight line through the center of the circle, being a third of that, comes to slightly more than ten and a half minor parts. Then, in drawing too, on the face of the image, straight across from ear to ear, it comes to just that many minor parts. The head, again, should be made in the shape of a parasol. The uṣṇīṣa at the center of the head of the Buddha, the Blessed One, is four digits in both height and width. Between the uṣṇīṣa and the forehead is eight digits. For the Blessed One glorious Cakrasaṃvara, the uṣṇīṣa is made at the center, and the topknot of matted hair is twelve digits. For the heroes it is eight or ten digits, and so too for the bodhisattvas.

In summary, the face is of four kinds, by the shapes of a hen's egg, a sesame seed, a circle and a square. That is stated:

A hen's egg, the shape of a sesame seed,
a square, together with a circle:
for all the ordinary figures
the shapes of the face are four.
For great beings and for goddesses,
the hen's egg and the sesame shape:
beautiful and lovely to behold,
the face should be made in these two.
For pretas, bhūtas, piśācas
and the wrathful ones, the altered shapes,
the circle and the square:
the face should be made in these two.

Here, for the characteristic of the face made in five places, setting aside four barley grains, one divides it into twelve, and in width, at the lower corners, by encircling and removing portions of one, one and a half, one, two and two and a half, there arise the principal, left, right and western faces of the Blessed One Cakrasaṃvara. The right face is like the faces of perfect buddhas, bodhisattvas and the like, shaped like a hen's egg, exceeding by four barley grains. The western face is like those of Vajravārāhī, Khaṇḍarohā and Rūpiṇī, in the shape of a sesame seed. The principal face is like that of Ḍākinī, in the shape of a circle. The left face is like those of Lāmā and of the heroes and the sovereign heroines, square.

Below the face the neck is four digits, eight digits straight across, and twenty-four digits around the middle. It has three creases: the first crease is two digits, the second one and a quarter digits, and the third one digit less a quarter. The first crease is at the root of the shoulders; it should be made so that from there upward the neck comes to just four digits. From the edge of the neck straight across, the shoulder is eight digits. Straight across beyond that, the head of the upper arm is four digits; this is what is called the shoulder. Both of these are rounded with swelling flesh, and being fleshy, the shoulder straight across is six digits, and likewise above the armpit. Below the armpit the arms are four palms in length.

There, beginning from the root of the armpit, the portion up to the elbow is twenty minor parts. Below the elbow and above the wrist, the forearm is sixteen minor parts. Below the wrist, the leaf of the hand is twelve minor parts. The elbow and the wrist are each one minor part. The circumferences of the upper arm, the forearm and the wrist are, in order, twenty, sixteen and twelve minor parts. In the leaf of the hand, the portion from the wrist to the root of the fingers is called the palm; its breadth straight across and its length are both seven minor parts. Beginning from the edge between the index finger and the thumb, its width, extending to the edge of the hand, is five minor parts. On it one should make lines in the shapes of a conch, a wheel and so on.

From the palm upward, the middle finger is five minor parts in length. Half a joint less than that is the index finger. Half a digit less than the middle finger is the ring finger. One joint less than that is the little finger. All have three joints, as is beautiful. The nail is half a joint. The thumb is four minor parts long from the wrist, and its circumference is also four. Half of the joint of its second joint is the nail. Thus one should make the thumb as it is, and the index finger comes to exceed it by three minor parts. Therefore from the root of the thumb to the root of the index finger is set at three minor parts. The little finger, again, is placed at a distance of five digits from the wrist. The circumference at the tip of the hand is also twelve digits. In circumference, the fingers at the root are less by a third of their own length; at the middle, less by a third of the root joint; at the tip, less by a third of the middle joint. What is being said is that, beginning from their roots, one should make the fingers gradually tapering and very round, as is beautiful.

From the neck to the heart is twelve and a half minor parts, and from the heart to the navel it is the same. Of the round of the breast, apart from the width of about a barley grain of what is known as the nipple, the rest of such a round breast likewise has the same extent. Here the width of the round side of the nipple is four barley grains all around; some say it is a width of two barley grains. From the breast upward to the root of the armpit is a distance of six digits. From the armpit upward, the portion extending to the end of the shoulder is nine minor parts. From armpit to armpit the chest is twenty-five digits. At the root of the armpit, horizontally and straight, it is eight digits. The circumference all around, where the chest and the roots of the armpits are level, is sixty-six digits.

The part below the armpit, which is called by the word "side," is twelve minor parts in width straight across from the back to the nipple. The width spanning both sides, level with the root of the breasts, is said to be twenty-two and a half minor parts. All around, it is sixty-nine digits. At the end of the heart the width is twenty minor parts, and likewise at the back; on each of the two sides it is ten minor parts, so its circumference is sixty minor parts. The third region, slightly rounded, is fifteen minor parts at the middle, and its circumference is forty-five minor parts. Level with the navel the circumference is forty-six minor parts. Slightly above the navel, horizontally, straight across from the breasts as far as between the nipples, is sixteen digits. From the hollow of the throat to the nipple is twelve digits. The navel is one minor part all around, coiling to the right, and one minor part deep.

Setting aside eight digits below the navel, the waist is eighteen minor parts in width straight across; some say nineteen minor parts. Its circumference is threefold. From the navel to the male organ is one face. The male organ is half a face in both length and circumference. The testicles are five minor parts in length and four minor parts in width straight across at the root. These two are to be produced by the mind alone, because the Blessed One's private parts are withdrawn into a sheath. At the back the buttocks are four minor parts all around. The round of the swelling buttocks is eight digits; some say three digits.

From the root of the testicles to the end of the knee, the thigh is twenty-five minor parts; the circumference at its root is thirty-six digits, and at the middle thirty-two digits. The knee is twenty-eight digits. The segment at the place joining the top of the knee and the shank measures three digits. Above and below this, at the junctions where the thigh and shank connect, there are two minor parts each. Below that, the shank, extending to the ankle, is twenty-five minor parts. At its middle the circumference is twenty-one minor parts, and at the tip fourteen minor parts. What is being said by these is that it should taper gradually, from the circumference of thirty-six minor parts at the root of the thigh to fourteen minor parts at the tip of the shank. That is stated:

The root of the thigh, its middle,
above the knee and the place of the knee,
below it and the round of the shank,
the end of the shank and above the ankle:
reduce each one, starting from a palm,
until five digits remain.
Thus the widths of all of these,
tripled, give their circumferences.

From the tip of the shank, the ankle is two minor parts in both length and breadth, and it is not prominent. At the ankle, the foot projecting forward is two minor parts. At the tip, the width straight across is half of that. At the middle it is five minor parts. At the root, at the place of the heel, it is four minor parts. At the root of the big toe the height of the foot is two digits. In the middle, below the ankle, the height of the heel is four minor parts. Therefore from the root of the testicles to the tip of the heel the lower body is sixty-three digits. From the root of the testicles upward to the root of the uṣṇīṣa the upper body is sixty-three digits. From the root of the little toe to the root of the heel the height is one minor part. The heel, projecting from the root of the foot, is six minor parts. Therefore, in sum, the length of the foot is eighteen digits. From the root of the foot its circumference is likewise. There, the second joint of the big toe has a nail of half a joint; the big toe is five digits around and three digits long. The second toe is equal in length to the big toe. The middle toe is the second toe less a sixteenth.

The ring toe is the middle toe less an eighth. The little toe is that less an eighth. The toes beginning with the little toe are four, and their measure is their own length taken as circumference.

These, for the present, are the characteristics of the Buddha, the Blessed One, and of glorious Cakrasaṃvara; those of the bodhisattvas and heroes are just these as well. However, for figures of ten palms, one sets aside half a minor part from each palm and lays out the ten palms with a palm of twelve minor parts. Again, the Blessed Lady Vajravārāhī and the others are nine palms, with a palm of twelve digits; so too are divine women. Indra, Upendra, Varuṇa, Īśvara, Vāyu, Brahmā, the peaceful planets and the nāgas are short; and the wrathful ones with pendulous bellies, such as Yamāntaka, are of eight palms, as are Vemacitrin, Kubera, Nairṛti, Yama, the fire god and the fierce planets. Vetālas are of twelve palms. Among these, the peaceful ones have faces like a hen's egg, and the fierce ones have square faces. The rest are as before. Following these, one should lay out the minor parts for each, and the learned should judge for themselves and understand the hips and limbs of the body, their thickness and slenderness and grace and so on, as is beautiful.

The Blessed Lady's body of fifty-four minor parts has its upper and lower halves. From the root of the hair to the end of the navel is forty-two minor parts, and the circumference level with the navel is forty-six minor parts. At the tips of the breasts it is twenty-eight minor parts. Following this, for the cheeks, nose, neck, shoulders, upper arms, armpits, arms, roots of the thighs and so on, it is somewhat slenderer and broader than the characteristics in minor parts stated for the Blessed One, as is beautiful. It is also stated:

Taking the measure equally from eye to eye,
the shanks, the thighs and the place of the waist
are rightly taken at the middle from the length;
straight across from the part, the digits likewise.
The two breasts should be carefully judged,
round, firm, high and full;
from their fullness the digits of the face
are to be taken, judged from the measure of height.
Rightly take the height of the nose;
place the pair of eyebrows in position.
At the root, for the sake of fullness, the goddess,
seen from the front, just as before:
on the cheeks, on the side of the root,
take the digits for the fullness,
divide them in two and set them out;
likewise the forehead on the face.
The height of the cheekbones, six portions:
taking these, set them out on the eyes.
Straight across from the length, half of that too;
the ears hang down, the digits likewise.
The root of the hair should be left aside;
the part of the forehead is a fourth,
and two portions should be made.
It is less than the measure of the seat of the senses;
the beauty is less by half the seat of the senses.
The shoulders should be well made;
and the neck too, its digits reduced,
made straight across and set above.
From the portions of the two forearms,
taking two digits in order,
from the thighs, knees and shanks,
fullness should be carefully judged.
In the height of the feet too,
and level with the soles of the feet likewise,
rightly taking two parts,
produce it rightly from the length of the upper arm.
The buttocks full and round:
make their own measure from the digits;
rightly take seven portions,
and judge those same ones for the length.
For the rest, as it has been taught here,
judge it fully by the measure of a woman.
High shoulders and beautiful sides,
a luster very clear and smooth,
lower lip like a bimba fruit, wide eyes:
this is famed as a woman.

"Crooked" means crooked. "Hollow" means thin. "Squint-eyed" means the eyes are crooked. "If the nose is impaired" means that the measures of the nose are impaired, and likewise those of the ears, lips and forehead. "Certainly the self" means certainly the very self of the yogins. "Breath gazing upward" means that the eyes and nostrils are turned upward. "By other powers that have become different" refers to thickness and thinness and posture being wrong. "From its own seat" means if its own seat is disturbed. "Inner hall" means that if the measures of the chest and so on become wrong, many faults are well known to follow. A crooked eye is an impaired measure, and so too with wrong signs and mudrās. In particular, the artist should be disciplined and well trained. "One should draw in good meditative equipoise" means that when drawing one should be in good meditative equipoise. The passage beginning "All the characteristics" means a rite for a beautiful form, complete in all the characteristics. "The image should also be given its mood," it says, in the passage beginning "Peaceful." The intent is that a deity should be drawn just in accordance with whatever mood predominates in it, and not the reverse. This is the explanation of the thirtieth chapter.

Yogins abiding in both stages should also rely on that very wisdom. And since, if she is not pleased, she will not grant accomplishment, in order to please her fully the Teacher spoke the passage beginning "Then." "With the binding of the lotus" means with the binding of the lotus posture. "Touch like a lotus" means that her limbs are very soft. "Touch in the shape of a pill" means within the lotus. "Cow's tongues" means rough within the lotus. "Verses, poetry, cleanliness" means delighting in verses and the like, in what is pure in word and meaning.

On the point that the yogin who relies on a mudrā should discern the true movement of the winds and the mind, the Teacher spoke, in the passage beginning "Then," a teaching of the distinctive characteristics of the four cakras, the divisions of transference stated in accord with the fifth chapter. The inner self is the subtle transference of the winds and of the awakening mind. And since that is hard to discern without the yoga of caṇḍālī, in order to point it out he spoke the passage beginning "At the crown." "At the crown" means at the uṣṇīṣa. "Place of liquor" means at the cakra of great bliss. "Outside" means below it. "Awakening" is the nature of the full moon disk, with fifteen digits, whose nature is the awakening mind. "Yoginī" means the sixteenth digit, the Blessed Lady. "On the two sides" means on its two sides, lalanā and rasanā, whose nature is the vowels and consonants. "Fruit": each later one is the fruit and each earlier one the cause; these are the four joys. "By the principal one itself" means the nature of innate joy.

For that very reason that supreme sovereign lady of nonduality is also twofold, as he stated in the passage beginning "Conventional." The conventional is the descending awakening mind, white like a kunda flower. "Unobscured" means the ultimate, which is nonconceptual, by nature mere bliss. He stated the nature common to both in the passage beginning "The Buddha": it is the support of the aggregates, elements and sense sources. "Vajra holder" means the vajra of the awakening mind. "From that" means from that very one. "Brahmā's egg" means from the utter emptiness in the center. "Of self-arisen wisdom": this is, conventionally, the state of support and supported. "In the lotus" means into the lotus. "In the place" means at the place. "In the seventy-two thousand" is the seventh case used in the sense of the sixth. "All" means "of all." In order to teach this, which is principal by way of the conventional and the ultimate, he says, "I pay homage to those blessed ones." The passage beginning "By whom" teaches that very thing as principal. Or else, this is the reasoning of the stages of cultivating the path that has been explained and will be explained.

He teaches the ultimate in particular in the passage beginning "Entity." The wisdom that is impelled by analysis through the characteristic of being free of the nature of one or many in the collection of aggregates, elements and sense sources is itself the wind. Set in motion by it, Caṇḍikā is made to blaze; the clear and the very clear become the antidote to suffering, equanimity and darkness; and great bliss, its weapons blazing, radiates and spreads everywhere. That is the perfectly knowing, stainless one. When does this become clear? He states it in the passage beginning "Burned": when the conceptions of the aggregates and the rest have subsided. He states this very point: "Objectless, perfectly knowing." That which, in this way, possesses the state in which all things pervading the sphere of space have been cultivated as of one taste is the nectar. The intent is that what has an object abides in one part and declines.

First one should know the rite of outer joy, and the melting brought about by the wisdom consort herself; for that purpose he spoke the passage beginning "Then." "Form of the drop" means having the form of a drop. "Just as it is": the distinction is this. "Left" means that in the bright fortnight it climbs from the big toe to the crown along the left side of the body, and in the dark fortnight it descends from the crown to the big toe along the right side of the body. Through the application of waxing and waning in their order, this is the part conventionally called moon and sun. Since it waxes in order and is hard to discern, it is subtle in nature; since it wanes, it is coarse in nature. So it should be established. Observing the places according to the order of the semen, desire and lunar days, in each of those places, upon each of those letters, one should visualize a moon resting on a sun, with a drop and a blaze, its head turned upward, and one should bring about melting by kissing, squeezing, scratching with the nails and the like.

If one asks how the digits of the moon and the sixteen transitions are, he states it in the passage beginning "Awakening." Beyond the division of these into lunar days, two kinds of transition are also taught, by the division of inner places and by the division of the shift between the nostrils; they are coarse and subtle in nature. Together with the wind, separately above and below, in the very form of semen, the moon moves on the left and the right. And that movement is hard for those in meditative equipoise to discern: that is the subtle one. If one asks how the sixteen transitions are, he states it in the passage beginning "Moon," by which moon and sun come to be held. From what does that come? He states: "Stopping the drop and stopping space." Through the wind moving outside and entering inside, the moon is dried up; therefore, by that rite one closes the openings of the channels in which it moves. "One endowed with these applications should know the sixteen transitions": that is the summary. Or else, for the yogin supremely certain of the sixteen transitions as they have been taught, moon and sun cease. Then the drop ceases. Then "space," meaning consciousness, ceases and dissolves into bliss, which has no proximate cause of conceptual elaboration. Thus: "Fully endowed with this reasoning, The sixteen transitions are set forth." He states this very point in the passage beginning "Nonconceptual": "Having the form of the wisdom manifestly desired." These alone are the nature of wisdom, bliss free from conceptuality, beyond doubt. Or else, the meaning of "without equal" is that the wisdom free of any further dependence does not arise from contact with the external. Because it is the cause from which ever more excellent states arise, outwardly there is no completion and no final end. Is there then no intent regarding the external? He states it in the passage beginning "Joy": the intent is that it becomes a means of entering inwardly into the house of equality and bliss.

The explanation of the thirty-first chapter.

To answer the question, "How is the intoxicating torma principal?" he spoke the passage beginning "Then." "Maṇḍala" means at the time of beginning the drawing of the maṇḍala, the fire offering, mantra recitation and the like. "Intoxicating torma" means if there is no torma furnished with liquor and the substance of semen. "Protection" means the two armors of the Blessed One and the Blessed Lady. "And proclaiming the sound of the syllable hūṃ" means preceded by uttering the three syllables. "Instant" is said with reference to the meditator of the completion stage and others. "Mantra" means the one beginning "oṃ sarba bīra."

The meaning is this. In all cases, such as when beginning meditation, one is first instantly in the yoga of two-armed Cakrasaṃvara. Then one utters the three mantras, "oṃ āḥ hūṃ sarba bīra yogīnīnāṃ kāya bāk citta bajra svabhāba ātmako'haṃ" and "oṃ bajra śuddhaḥ sarba dharmā bajra śuddho'haṃ," and in that very instant one intensely imagines the purification of the aggregates, elements and sense sources, the two armors, and at the twenty-four places of the body the heroes and the twenty-four sovereign heroines and the rest, all of them, complete without exception. In front one makes a maṇḍala of fragrant perfume and the like, and duly worships the wheel of deities. If one asks what is to be done then, he spoke the passage beginning "First," which is easy to understand. In the passage beginning "samaya stvaṃ," at the end of "one should please the deities," this "yathā sukhaṃ" should be understood as following without a break.

Here the meaning is this. Having prepared the torma in the order that has been taught, one should invite the Blessed One with the net mudrā and its mantra, and at exactly the same time, with the mantras beginning "indra," Indra and the others together with their retinues. Having worshipped them, beginning with offering water and the like, one admits them as is fitting into the wheel of commitment. One sets down the torma vessel, or imagines it, upon a vajra handful arisen from hūṃ, formed by one's left hand and the other joined together, which are of the nature of moon and sun. One utters the mantra beginning "samaya," and also recites the verse beginning "Goddess." Then one sees the deities drinking the nectar, drawn up through tubes of light rays coming forth from a single-pointed vajra arisen from hūṃ. Circling with leftward and rightward circlings, one should offer the torma to the Blessed One and the Blessed Lady with the mantra beginning "oṃ kara," and also to the deities from Ḍākinī through to Yamamathanī with the mantra beginning "oṃ bajra."

The passage beginning "Then" is easy to understand. "The eight-line" is easy to understand. Having offered betel and the like to all of them, one should praise the Blessed One with the eight-line mantra and make supplications, such as requesting the aims one desires. "Sign" is the word of the brief teaching; the passage beginning "oṃ" is the extensive teaching. Some say that the mudrā of the three banners is the "hand of the sign." "After that" refers to the particular leftover torma that belongs only to the occasion of the feast gathering. Here one collects the food, vegetables and the like that are in all the vessels and places them in a single vessel, and offers flowers, incense and the like. The master fills it with liquor, sets it facing forward with the net mudrā, and utters the syllables "hā pūṃ" of the substance that lies in its opening; sprinkling the torma vessel with that, one should give it with the mantra beginning "oṃ sarba kha khā." It should be carried to a solitary place such as a charnel ground: that is what "by the karmavajra" means. "Compassion" means that one should sing vajra songs at the feast gathering.

If one asks by whom the rites of pacifying and the rest are accomplished, he states it in the passage beginning "All aspects." The meaning is: of the yogin who meditates on glorious Cakrasaṃvara and Vajravārāhī, who possess the supreme of all aspects, who are of the nature of purified great bliss, and who are the embodiment of skillful means and wisdom. "Perfect awakening" means that the merit of performing the feast gathering and the like should be dedicated to suchness, the nature of the awakening mind. "Each day" means that if one has the means, the feast gathering should be performed every day or every month; and even if one does not, it should without fail be performed once each year.

The patron offers fees and the like, and the lord of the maṇḍala and the members of the maṇḍala assemble and pronounce a virtuous benediction for his sake, with this verse beginning "Generosity and so on." It connects with "May this glory be yours." Who are they? "Who" means whoever, and "turns the wheel" means fully turns it. In what form? "With Heruka and the other ḍākinīs" means in the forms of glorious Cakrasaṃvara, glorious Vajravārāhī and the rest. Having done what? "Beings": having completely fulfilled the hopes in the minds of beings. And again, what? Destroying all the cities of conceptuality and delusion, and establishing those to be trained in the three kinds of awakening according to their fortune; or else, "having himself attained buddhahood." "Victor, guru" means Vajrasattva. Being nondual with wisdom, he is the source of wisdom; "and this" means the source of wisdom. The meaning of the verse is this: having completed the two accumulations through the perfections of generosity and the rest, and having fully realized unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, the Blessed One, glorious Vajrasattva, in the form of the principal deities such as glorious Cakrasaṃvara embraced by Vajravārāhī, and of the circle of the maṇḍala, works individually and again and again for the benefit of the sentient beings of all the three realms without exception. Whatever virtue is his, and that assembly of unequaled qualities such as the powers and the fearlessnesses, which have the nature of Maheśvara: through their being pleased, may it be so for you as well.

The explanation of the thirty-second chapter.

In order to answer the question beginning "All," he spoke the passage beginning "Then." "Wisdom" means that the arising of wisdom has become clear. Accomplishment is the excellent and final state; just as its vow is stated in brief, so it is. "Various" means the various kinds of ascertaining and attaining. "Manner": by what is that fruit ascertained? By what means? So it is. He states this very point in the passage beginning "Accomplishment." The chapter on the completion stage was the brief teaching, and the chapter ascertaining reality the extensive explanation; this is the very extensive explanation. "All thoughts are unthinkable": because all sentient beings are inconceivable. "Unthinkable": because free of thought. "How": because there is no difference between all sentient beings, the mind of the yogin, and the tathāgatas.

"Through being unthinkable" means through being without mind. "Of the one who thinks the inconceivable" means thinking in a way that cannot be thought. "Not various superimpositions" means that there is nothing with superimposition, and also nothing free of superimposition.

Here the instruction is this. The yogin who has gone first through the generation stage and has become thoroughly habituated up to the culmination of the stage of vajra recitation sits cross-legged in some place, and should make it fully evident that in the three realms not the slightest thing is established as a real entity, and that they are pervaded by the nature of glorious Vajravārāhī and Cakrasaṃvara, the embodiment of wisdom and skillful means. Having cleared away every conception of what does not exist, if one rests without conceptuality, exactly so, through the stage of luminosity according to the guru's instruction, before long nonconceptual wisdom will become clear. And even after rising from that, regarding all stable and moving things, which are like a dream and free of distinct intrinsic natures, one should view them thus: "O, what is there to take up and what to reject?"

The passage beginning "Dream" teaches these things. The yogin who comes to abide free of conceptuality has, just so, the bliss of self-awareness, and that itself is the perfection of wisdom. Realization abides only in the fruit of joy, and that itself is free of intrinsic nature. These very two are wisdom and skillful means, and also emptiness and compassion. These two are qualities of the one mind alone: the realization of great bliss, which is itself without intrinsic nature. In this way, by meditating on this in both states and enjoying the five sense pleasures, the accumulations are brought to completion. If one asks how one can see this as like a dream and the rest and still engage in it in order to enjoy it, he states it in the passage beginning "Illusion."

In order to teach, by way of summing up the nature of the four joys, the Blessed One who pervades all the three realms without exception, who is the fruit, the culmination of habituation to the two stages taught in this way together with their branches, he spoke the passage beginning "Emaho, great bliss!" "Eat" means enjoy. "Object" means in the aspect of the apprehended; "like a young woman's dream" means like a young woman seeing in a dream that she has found a son. All of these must be correctly understood from the guru alone: so he says in the passage beginning "All offerings." There he states the characteristics of a disciple who is a vessel for the instructions, in the passage beginning "Fear": "Removing fear and wrongdoing," which means having abandoned fear and wrongdoing. He states the greatness of the tantra in the passage beginning "Glorious." If this alone is the supreme path, why are other practices also taught? He states it in the passage beginning "Various inclinations." Therefore even those who have no special inclination toward this should not disparage this way: so he says in the passage beginning "By the profound." He states the reason in the passage beginning "Inconceivable." "The nature of all things" means the nature of the reality of all buddhas, whose characteristic is the perfect accomplishment of the aims of beings. It is inconceivable because it is immeasurable; the intent is, even for bodhisattvas. It is inconceivable also in this sense: knowing "emptiness and compassion inseparable," this is surely the play of the buddhas. As it is also said: "Emptiness and compassion inseparable, That on which the mind intently meditates: That itself is the teaching Of the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha." The intent is that Buddhists should hold to and adopt the statement that here too this very thing is taught in the form of glorious Heruka, the wisdom of inseparable emptiness and compassion, encircled by the wheel of ḍākinīs.

If one asks in what manner, for what purpose, and as what the other paths are rightly taught, he states it in the passage beginning "Sentient beings": it is in order to lead sentient beings in. "Inclination" means "by these paths of those who are inclined to them"; the remainder is "he taught them as pleases all of them." In their own nature, they are those who abide in the nature of great bliss, the samādhi of glorious Heruka encircled by all the heroines. By these words it is made clear that even those who enter by other paths, at the end, abide in the fruit that is the culmination of the path itself.

That is the concluding summary spoken by the compiler, the passage beginning "spoke." From the Abhidhāna Tantra of glorious Heruka, consisting of three hundred thousand lines, through the special way of summarizing its entire meaning, and in order to teach here innate joy, the later tantra, the short tantra, and the explanatory tantra as well, called the Kalpa of the Arising of the Innate, also named the Saṃvarodaya, was extracted, in order to care for those to be trained who are born in the time of the five degenerations.

At my students' request, following what I have heard, I have spoken, not from pride or any such motive;
Whatever faults I have produced here, glorious Vajravārāhī, please forgive.
One born blind, without a companion, cannot keep steady on a path hard to travel;
Moment by moment he goes astray and falls into doubt, and so deserves forbearance.
Moreover, even a child's meaningless and jumbled words
Are dear to its mother and give rise to nothing but delight.
Keeping this in mind, by whatever virtue I have gained from speaking this,
May all here without exception accomplish perfect awakening.
Touched by the sun of the sublime instructions that have come from one accomplished master and another,
This lotus pond of my mind, the Padminī, has blossomed; drawn here and there by its sweet fragrance,
Those who through delusion have wandered long, without leisure, and grown weary,
May that swarm of fortunate bees, their minds held by it, be filled everywhere with joy.

The Difficult Points Commentary on the King of Tantras, the Glorious Saṃvarodaya, called Padminī, composed by the great scholar who attained accomplishment, the monk Ratnarakṣitapāda, is complete.

His direct disciple, Sarvajñaśrībhadra, had translated it on his own. From that, later, at the command of the holy lama, the Dharma king, endowed with boundless qualities, and urged by the words of the great official Kunga Zangpo, the Tibetan translator, the monk Lodrö Ten, translated it, revised it and corrected it at the temple of Puri.

The profound meaning of emptiness and compassion
Is a seal bound with vajra words.
Though one like me cannot break it open,
What contradiction is there in entering with devotion?
By the sunlight of the virtue of this effort,
May the stream of the Padminī's honey
Be given in due season, without obstacle,
To all beings, the bees, without exception.

Translator's notes

The opening verse plays on the image of the milk ocean: "the awakened" (Tibetan rnam sad, Sanskrit vibudha) also means "the gods," and the jewels, nectar and heroines echo the treasures churned from the ocean of milk. In "in its connections and its order," the text reads 'bral pa ("separation"); I have read it as 'brel pa ("connection"), which fits the context. The phrase "through the lord alone" is literal; it appears to refer back to the verse's "lord of speakers," meaning that naming the speaker alone can confer authority. "King of Tantras in Two Examinations" is the Hevajra Tantra. "Uncommon Secret Yoga Tantra" renders the Tibetan title literally; I have not identified it with certainty. Vajrapāṇipāda is given as the Tibetan reads, the honorific attached to the name Vajrapāṇi; the work referred to is not specified here. The final quotation from Jñānapāda is terse, and its second line could also be read as "the effort of meditation is great awakening." e vaṃ mayā śrutaṃ are the opening words of the tantra, "Thus have I heard," here read syllable by syllable in an esoteric sense. "The Lady of Action" renders las can ma literally; it is not the usual name for the consort of the earth element (normally Locanā), and I have not identified it with certainty. "Understood as the beloved" renders 'dod mar dran, which is uncertain. The quoted verse "Because the body of the perfect Buddha radiates" is Ratnagotravibhāga 1.28. pratisena refers to the Indian practice of divination in which a young girl sees images in a mirror or similar surface. The Ornament of the Sūtras verse is Mahāyānasūtrālaṃkāra 1.12; the Mahāyāna Uttaratantra is the Ratnagotravibhāga. "Grammatical gender and the like should be regarded as reversed" refers to the irregular grammar of the tantra's Sanskrit, which the commentator says must be read for the meaning. "The five paths" are presumably channels or courses of the winds in the body; the text does not specify them here. The commentator glosses the root tantra's wording, sometimes its grammar (case endings), so several quoted phrases are deliberately rendered close to the root's wording. "Vowel signs" renders gug skyed, apparently the written diacritics of mantra syllables. "Introductory setting" renders gleng gzhi (nidāna). The embryonic stages (nur nur po and the following) are given by their standard Sanskrit names, kalala and so on. "Realm of entities" renders dngos po'i yul; its precise reference in the objector's list is unclear. "And, on examination, as the completion stage" renders brtag pa rdzogs pa'i rim pa, whose construction is somewhat uncertain. The verse "Whatever is the limit of nirvāṇa" corresponds to Nāgārjuna's Root Verses on the Middle Way 25.20; the second verse is from the Hevajra Tantra. The embryonic stages are given by their standard Sanskrit names (arbuda, peśī, ghana). "Nutritive essences" renders ro (Skt. rasa). In the Āryadeva verse each line is read as "this is X, not Y"; "Lord of the Wheel" (Cakreśvara) may refer to the central deity of the maṇḍala. The commentator's remark on the Hevajra verse is compressed; it seems to mean that the bliss praised here is bliss inseparable from emptiness, so the Hevajra's denial that elemental bliss is reality does not conflict with it. In "the ālaya consciousness and the afflicted mind" the Tibetan word order is slightly displaced; the evident sense is translated. The passage on "entity and non-entity" is terse and its construal is tentative. "It is not to be expressed" follows the evident reading brjod par bya ba min; the text as written has yin ("is to be expressed"), but the following gloss and quotation show the negative is meant. In "the wheels of all phenomena and of great bliss at the heart and the crown," the expected pairing is the Dharma wheel at the heart and the great bliss wheel at the crown. "Accomplishment of Wisdom" renders Ye shes grub pa (Jñānasiddhi). "The fourth, consisting of two lines" renders a terse phrase (rkang pa gnyis kyi bzhi pa) whose exact reference in the root tantra is not certain. "If they are suppressed by the elements, nothing at all will be born" follows the Tibetan literally; the sense of "suppressed" here is unclear. The seven limbs are the seven features of union (complete enjoyment, union, great bliss, absence of intrinsic nature, compassion, uninterruptedness, unceasingness). "What has the nature of the nine" is taken as the nine levels of existence (the desire realm, the four form concentrations and the four formless attainments), which explains the commentator's remark about matter in the formless realm. "The Permanent One" renders rtag pa literally, as one of the three deities beside Amitābha and Akṣobhya; in the body, speech and mind scheme one would expect Vairocana, so the reading may be corrupt. "Establishes" renders the uncertain verb steng bar mdzad in the quoted passage on Kṣitigarbha. The authority quoted as Thag pa'i zhabs, literally "the master Rope," has not been identified with confidence, so the name is given in English. The units are the breath, the quarter breath, the pala (here reckoned at six breaths) and the daṇḍa; the figures given have been checked and are internally consistent (for example 50 palas, 3 breaths and 3 quarters equals 303 and three quarters breaths). "Eleven" renders drag po, "the fierce one," used here as a number word for the eleven Rudras; the arithmetic (eleven and a quarter twice making twenty-two and a half) confirms it. The difference the objector gives between 9,700 and 8,640 breaths is stated in the text as 1,110, which does not match exactly; it is translated as written. The astronomer Bhasata is given as the Tibetan transcribes the name and has not been identified. The King of the Two Examinations is the Hevajra Tantra. The text reads "sixty-two daṇḍas" at the opening but "thirty-two daṇḍas" a few lines later; both are translated as written. "Watch" renders thun in its sense of a division of the day (prahara), not a meditation session or ritual substance. "The first case in the sense of the sixth" is a grammatical gloss: a nominative used with genitive meaning. The figure "two hundred and a quarter" for one maṇḍala's breaths is rendered literally; the arithmetic of the following reckoning is not fully transparent. "The five paths" reads la ma lnga in the text, evidently for lam lnga ("five paths"), the wording of the question just cited. The quoted verse lists channels known from the Hevajra Tantra (Trivṛttā, Kāminī, Gehā, Caṇḍikā, Māradārikā); the Tibetan here gives the last as "Deathless Daughter," which the commentary plays against "daughters of Māra," so it is kept in translation. The phrases "the beings ... into the charnel ground; that into the samaya wheel" appear to be cited steps of a dissolution sequence and are rendered literally. In the Vajramālā citation the text reads nad du ("in illness"), evidently a slip for nang du ("inwardly"), which the sense of the contrast with "outwardly" requires; translated accordingly. "Jālandhara" here is the first of the sacred places mapped onto the body.
